Explorations in Scalar Fermion Theories: β \beta -functions, Supersymmetry and Fixed Points Preprint: DO-TH 22/06 arXiv: 2301.10903
Ian Jack, Hugh Osborn, and Tom Steudtner

Abstract
Results for β \beta -functions and anomalous dimensions in general scalar fermion theories are presented to three loops. Various constraints on the individual coefficients for each diagram following from supersymmetry are analysed. The results are used to discuss potential fixed points in the ε \varepsilon -expansion for scalar fermion theories, with arbitrary numbers of scalar fields, and where there are just two scalar couplings and one Yukawa coupling. For different examples the fixed points follow a similar pattern as the numbers of fermions is varied. For diagrams with subdivergences there are extensive consistency constraints arising from the existence of a perturbative a a -function and these are analysed in detail. Further arbitrary scheme variations which preserve the form of β \beta functions and anomalous dimensions in terms of 1PI diagrams are also discussed. The existence of linear and quadratic scheme invariants is demonstrated and the consistency condition are shown to be expressible in terms of these invariants.



1
Introduction

Whatever the role of supersymmetry in the phenomenological description of the world
at accessible energies there is no doubt that supersymmetric quantum field theories in
various dimensions have enhanced our understanding of quantum field theories
more generally. This is especially true non perturbatively where the duality between different
theories was first developed and the existence of conformal fixed points in three and higher
dimensions is much better understood. However there are also constraints at the perturbative level
where supersymmetric non-renormalisation theorems have implications for more general
\beta
-functions
and related quantities when they are reduced to the supersymmetric case.

In this paper we explore these, and other, constraints for general scalar fermion theories in four space-time dimensions
at up to three loops.
For pure scalar theories, this is hardly state of the art as general three loop results have been known for more than 30 years
Jack:1990eb
, and higher orders are available
Jack:2018oec
;
Kazakov
;
Steudtner:2020tzo
;
Bednyakov:2021ojn
.
Nevertheless, the corresponding expressions for general scalar fermion theories, allowing for arbitrary Yukawa couplings,
have only been obtained quite recently
Steudtner:2021fzs
;
Bednyakov:2021qxa
;
Davies:2021mnc
. While field anomalous dimensions and Yukawa
\beta
-functions were obtained, these depended on results already found
for a variety of special cases. The general
\beta
- and
\gamma
-functions are expressed in terms of contractions of generalised coupling tensors with each term corresponding to a specific allowed Feynman diagram at each loop order.
The associated results for the quartic
scalar
\beta
-function at three loops have not previously been fully determined
Steudtner:2021fzs
.
Closing this gap would also represent a stepping stone towards complete three-loop renormalisation group equations of any renormalisable QFT, which is now feasible after recent advances in general four-loop gauge and three-loop Yukawa results
Poole:2019kcm
;
Bednyakov:2021qxa
;
Davies:2021mnc
. Without gauge interactions, each term corresponds to a one particle irreducible (1PI) diagram, whose numbers increase rapidly with each loop order. With the quartic scalar coupling in standard regularisation schemes all
one vertex reducible diagrams (or
snail diagrams
) can be omitted so that the necessary diagrams are one
vertex irreducible (1VI).
The unknown coefficients may be partially fixed with direct calculations, e.g.
Chetyrkin:2012rz
;
Bednyakov:2013eba
;
Chetyrkin:2013wya
;
Bednyakov:2013cpa
;
Bednyakov:2014pia
;
Zerf:2017zqi
;
Mihaila:2017ble
in our case.
However, their number can be greatly reduced and literature results cross-checked by applying constraints
arising from special cases such as supersymmetry, which is the exercise undertaken here.
We are then able to fully determine the three loop beta function
for the quartic scalar couplings in general scalar fermion theories.

To carry out our analysis for general four-dimensional renormalisable scalar fermion theories,
it is natural to consider a basis with
n_{s}
real scalars
\phi^{a}
and essentially
n_{f}
pseudo-real
Majorana fermions
\psi
where the couplings are just
a symmetric 4 index real tensor
\lambda^{abcd}
and a Yukawa coupling
y^{a}
which is a symmetric

n_{f}\times n_{f}
real matrix
in the non spinorial fermion indices (which are here suppressed)
Steudtner:2021fzs
;
Poole:2019kcm
.
The discussion in subsequent sections then
concerns the beta functions
\beta_{\lambda}{\!}^{abcd},\ \beta_{y}{\!}^{a}
as well as associated anomalous
dimensions
\gamma_{\phi}{\!}^{ab},
and
\gamma_{\psi}
. These quantities completely determine
\beta
-functions when superrenormalisable couplings, corresponding to operators with dimension three or less,
are introduced, if background field methods are used and
\beta_{\lambda}{\!}^{abcd}
is extended
to
\beta_{V}(\phi)
for an arbitrary quartic scalar potential
V
. Equivalently by applying the so called dummy field technique
Martin:1993zk
;
Luo:2002ti
;
Schienbein:2018fsw
. The results here encompass those for Dirac
fermions, the corresponding reduction is described later.

For each fermion loop graph which leads to a trace over products
of the Yukawa coupling matrices
y^{a}
then with our conventions the numerical coefficient for each such trace
for a four-dimensional four-component Majorana spinor
\Psi

should have an additional factor 2 times the results quoted here. Such four-dimensional
Majorana spinors reduce in three dimensions to
two two-component real spinors which belong to inequivalent representations of the three-dimensional
Dirac algebra.

General theories of course can be restricted by imposing symmetries. With complex fields,
n_{s}
even,
then we may take

y^{a}\to(y^{i},{\bar{y}}_{i})
with
y^{i},{\bar{y}}_{i}
not necessarily square matrices but related by hermitian conjugation.
Imposing a
U(1)
symmetry where both scalar and fermion fields carry a charge, so that
all lines in any diagram are directed, the number of diagrams is significantly reduced (for the three loop 1PI
Yukawa vertex diagrams from 52 to 12)
Jack:2013sha
.

As a special case the
U(1)
symmetric theory encompasses the Wess-Zumino theory with
{\mathcal{N}}=1

supersymmetry and four supercharges
Wess:1973kz
. In a superspace
formalism, for the renormalisable theory, there are complex chiral superfields
\Phi_{i}
,
{\bar{\Phi}}{}^{i}
,
with an overall
U(1)
symmetry and
the general couplings are given by a symmetric 3 index tensor
Y^{ijk}
and its conjugate
{\bar{Y}}_{ijk}
,
which determine the scalar quartic couplings. There are then very strong non-renormalisation theorems
Salam:1974jj
;
Grisaru:1979wc

which ensure that the
\beta
-functions
\beta_{Y}{}^{ijk}
,
\beta_{{\bar{Y}}\hskip 0.5ptijk}

are determined just in terms of the anomalous dimensions
\gamma_{\Phi},\ \gamma
.
Moreover, dedicated literature for such supersymmetric QFT’s is available to high orders
Jack:1996qq
;
Parkes:1985hh
;
Abbott:1980jk
;
Sen:1981hk
;
Avdeev:1982jx
;
Gracey:2021yvb
.
This yields conditions on the
\beta
-functions and anomalous dimensions
for an arbitrary scalar fermion theory but these do not significantly reduce the number of independent
terms
Steudtner:2021fzs
.

In three dimensions there are scalar-fermion theories with just two supercharges
Gates
. In a superfield formalism
the theory is described in terms of a real superfield
\Phi^{a}
and for current interest there are just real cubic couplings
given by the symmetric three index tensor
Y^{abc}
. Such theories can emerge at fixed points under
RG flow
Thomas
;
Grover:2013rc
;
Fei:2016sgs
;
Gies

and may be relevant for fixed point exponents in
some condensed matter systems. For a single scalar field and a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry
this is a supersymmetric version of the 3d Ising model.
For several scalar fields then extending the theory away from three dimensions it is possible
to set up an epsilon expansion determining potential fixed points and their associated
critical exponents
Liendo:2021wpo
.
The 3d supersymmetric Ising model has been explored
using the bootstrap
Bashkirov:2013vya
;
Rong:2018okz
;
Atanasov:2018kqw
;
Rong:2019qer
;
Atanasov:2022bpi
,
with extensions to several fields in
Rong:2019qer
.
Of course extending supersymmetric theories away from their natural integer dimension, even
just in a perturbative expansion, is potentially fraught with problems. Various Ward identities necessary for
supersymmetry are no longer valid. These relate contributions with different numbers of fermion loops
and depend on Fierz identities. However these problems do not arise at low loop order, up to three loops in our case.
A discussion of the four dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}=1
supersymmetry algebra
extended away from four dimensions using a form of dimensional reduction was given in
Bobev
.
The minimal three dimensional supersymmetric scalar-fermion theory
would define an apparent four dimensional theory with
{\mathcal{N}}=\tfrac{1}{2}
supersymmetry
1
1

1

This is different
from the
{\mathcal{N}}=\tfrac{1}{2}
supersymmetry discussed in
Seiberg:2003yz
;
Araki:2003se
which involve
non anti-commuting
\theta^{\prime}
s or
{\bar{\theta}}^{\prime}
s. The renormalisation
of these theories was considered in

Grisaru:2003fd
;
Britto:2003kg
;
Romagnoni:2003xt
;
Lunin:2003bm
;
Jack:2004pq
;
Jack:2007fb
;
Jack:2008ae
.
These theories of course do not exist as well defined Lorentz invariant unitary theories though there
exists the possibility of considering such a theory away from three dimensions where the full
d
-dimensional
Lorentz symmetry is broken. Dimensional
regularisation breaks supersymmetry but for
{\mathcal{N}}=1
theories such anomalous contributions breaking supersymmetric
Ward identities should be removable by an appropriate redefinition of the couplings, or essentially a change
of scheme. At one, two or three loops it is sufficient just to ensure that fermion traces are appropriately normalised.
We defer further discussion to the conclusion.

In section 8 we make use of the three loop results to discuss possible fixed points in the
\varepsilon
-expansion
for fermion scalar theories. We consider generalisations of the Gross-Neveu, Nambu Jona-Lasinio and Heisenberg
theories which have
n_{s}=1,2,3
scalar fields and have
O(n_{s})
symmetry. For
n_{s}\geq 4
there are theories
with reduced
H\subset O(n_{s})
symmetry which have two scalar couplings and one Yukawa coupling.
For a consistent RG flow with the reduced set of couplings it is necessary to impose completeness relations
on the matrices defining the Yukawa couplings. For square matrices we identify six different examples where
these are satisfied. In each case the numbers of fermions
n_{f}
can be arbitrarily large. For vanishing Yukawa
coupling these theories
generally have just the
O(n_{s})
and Gaussian fixed points though one example is equivalent to the scalar
theory with hypertetrahedral symmetry where there are two further fixed points with
{\cal S}_{n_{s}+1}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry. Assuming just lowest order
\beta
-functions the Yukawa
\beta
-function does not
contain the scalar couplings and is easily solved. For the scalar couplings there
are then relations between the fixed points for small and large
n_{f}
. A similar pattern emerges in each example.
Even if there are four fixed points when
n_{f}=0
these reduce to two except for very tiny or very large
n_{f}
.
Generally there are two fixed points for low and large
n_{f}
and for intermediate
n_{f}
either
0
or
4
.
These do not necessarily lead to scalar potentials which are bounded below, the Gaussian fixed point becomes
unstable when
n_{f}>0
, but there is a stable potential for large
n_{f}
related to the Gaussian fixed point as
n_{f}\to 0
.
We also consider
an example where the Yukawa matrices are not square, corresponding to chiral fermions, and where there is a

U(r)\times U(s)
symmetry and
n_{s}=rs
. The purely scalar theory may have four fixed points for suitable
r,s

but with a non zero Yukawa coupling there is a similar pattern.

Further constraints relating the coefficients
for the contributions of various diagrams to
\beta
-functions and anomalous dimensions
can be obtained from applying a perturbative
version of the
a
-theorem
Osborn:1989td
;
Jack:1990eb
;
Osborn:1991gm
;
Jack:2013sha
;
Poole:2019kcm
. In general this relates certain combinations of
\beta
and
\gamma
-function
coefficients at a particular loop order to lower order contributions. In the present context this
provides relations for the coefficients of the three loop Yukawa
\beta
-function and also
\gamma_{\phi}

and
\gamma_{\psi}
. Such conditions were analysed at length by Poole and Thomsen
Poole:2019kcm
, including also gauge
couplings. We present their results here without any explicit evaluation of lower order one and two
loop contributions so that the structure of the conditions is more apparent. We also consider the restriction to

U(1)
symmetry where results are more tractable.

The outline of the paper is as follows: In the next four sections we list the diagrams for the scalar and fermion anomalous dimensions and the Yukawa
and quartic scalar
\beta
-functions for the general scalar fermion theory at up to three loops. We also give
the values for the corresponding coefficients, 143 at three loops,
which are all consistent with the various relations obtained later. Of course at one and two loops
results have been known for a long time, we list the coefficients diagram by diagram.
Our conventions match those in
Poole:2019kcm
and our numerical results at one and two loops
agree precisely once they are multiplied by the required factor to ensure overall symmetry.
Similarly the three loop results for the Yukawa
\beta
-function and also the anomalous dimensions
agree exactly with
Davies:2021mnc
.
In the case of the quartic scalar
\beta
-function the relations obtained here are used to provide complete
results for all terms appearing in the general expansion. For simplicity the Yukawa couplings are rescaled by

4\pi
and the scalar quartic couplings by
16\pi^{2}
.
These coefficients all correspond to what would be obtained in a
\overline{M\!S}
scheme although no explicit
calculation is undertaken here.

The results are simplified in section 6 where a
U(1)
symmetry is imposed which significantly reduces the
number of terms present in the expansions of the general
\beta
-functions and anomalous dimensions.
The
U(1)
restriction contains as a special case
{\mathcal{N}}=1
supersymmetry and the various necessary linear
constraints are derived in section 7. We there also consider also the example of what is here termed

{\mathcal{N}}=\frac{1}{2}
supersymmetry where there are a significant number of linear constraints which are all
satisfied by the explicit results listed earlier in sections 2,3,4,5.

Besides supersymmetry conditions there are also relations for the various coefficients derived from
the existence of a perturbative
a
-function. We list the conditions for the general scalar-fermion theory
which are all derived from
Poole:2019kcm
. For the two loop anomalous dimensions and the Yukawa

\beta
-function there are 4 relations whereas at three loops there are 42. At three loops it is necessary
to also allow for 5 possible antisymmetric contributions to the anomalous dimensions and 4 relations
for these are obtained.

Section 8 contains our discussion of scalar fermion fixed points. For multiple scalars we show there
are theories which can be restricted to a single Yukawa coupling and two quartic scalar couplings.
As special cases these include the well known renormalisable Gross-Neveu and Nambu Jona-Lasinio
theories.

In general results for individual coefficients corresponding to particular diagrams are scheme dependent.
In section 10 scheme variations which preserve the structure in terms of contributions from 1PI diagrams
are considered. Coefficients corresponding to primitive diagrams, which have no subdivergences, are
individually invariant but this of course not true in general. We demonstrate how scheme invariants can be
formed and applied in detail to the three loop Yukawa
\beta
-function.
These can be linear or higher order in the coefficients. The scheme invariance of the
a
-function relations
is also verified.

Some further details are considered in various appendices. Appendix A describes a basis for Majorana fermions
relevant for reduction to three dimensions and their possible extensions away from
d=3
with broken Lorentz
invariance. In appendix B we outline some tensorial calculations relevant for the fixed point discussion.
Some figures elucidating how the fixed points in scalar fermion theories vary with differing numbers
of fermions are given in appendix C.
In appendix D we describe the derivation of the
a
-function relations at two and three loops
after restricting to
U(1)
symmetry, there is then one relation at two loops and 12 at three.
Finally in appendix E we discuss some general features of scheme changes
which preserve the perturbative structure in terms of contributions corresponding to 1PI diagrams.

so that

\mathrm{tr}(y^{a_{1}}y^{a_{2}}\dots y^{a_{p}})\big|_{\rm Majorana}=\big(1+(-1)^{p}\big)\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{a_{1}\dots a_{p}})\,.

(2)

The coefficients for the trace corresponding to a fermion loop then has an additional factor two and there
are only an even number of Yukawa couplings on any loop.

4
Yukawa Couplings

The Yukawa coupling
\beta
-function can be decomposed as

\beta_{y}^{a}=\beta_{y}^{a\hskip 0.5ptT}={\tilde{\beta}}_{y}^{a}+\gamma^{ab}_{\phi}\,y^{b}+\gamma_{\psi}\,y^{a}+y^{a}\,\gamma_{\psi}\,,

(11)

where
{\tilde{\beta}}_{y}^{a}
is determined solely by the contributions of 1PI diagrams.

At one and two loops the relevant 1PI diagrams are

1

2a

2b

2c

2d

2e

2f

Figure 5:
One and two loop Yukawa vertex diagrams.

where

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{y}^{(1)a}={}

\displaystyle\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\;y^{bab}\,,

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{y}^{(2)a}={}

\displaystyle\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}\,\lambda^{abcd}y^{bcd}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}\,y^{bac}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{bc})+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\,{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\,y^{baccb}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}\,{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\,y^{bacbc}

\displaystyle{}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}\,y^{bcacb}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}\,y^{bcabc}\,,

(12)

with
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\,y^{baccb}=y^{baccb}+y^{bccab}
, as necessary for symmetry.
Old results, with our conventions, give

\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}=2\,,\quad\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}=-2\,,\ \ \beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}=-1\,,\ \ \beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}=0\,,\ \ \beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}=-2\,,\ \ \beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}=2\,.

(13)

5
Scalar Quartic Couplings

The scalar quartic coupling is a symmetric 4 index tensor
\lambda^{abcd}
and the
\beta
-function
has a similar decomposition as for the Yukawa coupling in (
11
)

\beta_{\lambda}^{\,abcd}={\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda}^{\,abcd}+\gamma_{\phi}^{ae}\lambda^{ebcd}+\gamma_{\phi}^{be}\lambda^{aecd}+\gamma_{\phi}^{ce}\lambda^{abed}+\gamma_{\phi}^{de}\lambda^{abce}={\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda}^{\,abcd}+{\mathcal{S}}_{4}\,\gamma_{\phi}^{ae}\lambda^{ebcd}

(16)

with
{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda}^{\,abcd}
given in terms of 1PI diagrams and
{\mathcal{S}}_{4}
here denoting the
sum over the four terms, each term with unit weight, necessary to obtain a fully symmetric result.

At one and two loops the relevant diagrams are

1a

1b

2a

2b

2c

2d

2e

2f

2g

Figure 7:
One and two loop diagrams relevant for the scalar quartic
\beta
-function.

so that

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda}^{(1)abcd}={}

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1a}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{3}\,\lambda^{abef}\lambda^{cdef}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1b}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{3}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{abcd})\,,

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda}^{(2)abcd}={}

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{6}\,\lambda^{abef}\lambda^{cfgh}\lambda^{degh}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{3}\,\lambda^{abef}\lambda^{cdeg}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{fg})

\displaystyle{}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{6}\,\lambda^{abef}\mathrm{tr}(y^{efcd})+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{6}\,\lambda^{abef}\mathrm{tr}(y^{ecfd})

\displaystyle{}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{12}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{aeebcd})+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{12}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{aebcde})+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2g}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{6}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{eabecd})\,,

(17)

with
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
denoting the sum over
n
terms necessary to achieve symmetrisation over all
permutations of
a,b,c,d
.
Historic results, with our conventions, give

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1a}=1\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1b}=-4\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}=-1\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}=-1\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}=0\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}=2\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}=2\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}=4\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2g}=4\,.

\displaystyle{}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c^{\prime}}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{24}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{af\!febced})+{\mathcal{S}}_{12}\,\big(\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3d^{\prime}}\;tr(y^{abecf\!fde})+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3e^{\prime}}\;\mathrm{tr}(y^{aefebcfd})\big)

\displaystyle{}+{\mathcal{S}}_{6}\,\big(\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3h^{\prime}}\;\mathrm{tr}(y^{aefbcfed})+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3i^{\prime}}\;\mathrm{tr}(y^{aefbcefd})\big)+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3j^{\prime}}\;{\mathcal{S}}_{3}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{aebfcedf})\,.

(26)

Of the quartic scalar diagrams

;

5

Steudtner:2021fzs
without inconsistencies. Hence, literature results
Zerf:2017zqi

are cross-checked by the SUSY relations and explicit SM computations.

6
Reduction to
U(1)
Symmetry

For complex fields with a
U(1)
symmetry the number of diagrams is significantly reduced
2
2

2

This example
was considered in
Jack:2013sha
.
This restriction is achieved by taking
\phi^{a}=(\varphi_{i},{\bar{\varphi}}^{i})
, so that
\phi^{a}\phi^{\prime}{}^{a}=\varphi_{i}\,{\bar{\varphi}}^{\prime}{}^{i}+{\bar{\varphi}}^{i}\,\varphi^{\prime}{\!}_{i}
, and

\tfrac{1}{24}\,\lambda^{abcd}\,\phi^{a}\phi^{b}\phi^{c}\phi^{d}\to\tfrac{1}{4}\,\lambda_{ij}{}^{kl}\,{\bar{\varphi}}^{i}{\bar{\varphi}}^{j}\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}\,,\quad\phi^{a}y^{a}\to\varphi_{i}\,y^{i}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)+{\bar{\varphi}}^{i}\,{\bar{y}}_{i}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\,.

(28)

The scalar and fermion lines on each diagram then have arrows with the basic vertices for the Yukawa
couplings
y^{i},\,{\bar{y}}_{i}
represented by

,

and for the scalar quartic coupling
\lambda_{ij}{}^{kl}
by

.
The triangle graphs present for real couplings are no longer allowed. With this prescription then
for traces over the Yukawa couplings

\mathrm{tr}(y^{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}\dots a_{2n}})\to\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i_{1}}y^{i_{2}}{\bar{y}}_{i_{3}}\dots y^{i_{2n}})+\mathrm{tr}(y^{i_{2n}}\dots y^{i_{2}}{\bar{y}}_{i_{1}})\big)\,,\\
\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\mathrm{tr}(y^{i_{1}}{\bar{y}}_{i_{2}}y^{i_{3}}\dots{\bar{y}}_{i_{2n}})+\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i_{2n}}\dots{\bar{y}}_{i_{2}}y^{i_{1}})\big)\,.\end{cases}

(29)

In general, for the anomalous dimensions

\gamma_{\phi}{}^{ab}=\big(\gamma_{\varphi\,i}{}^{j},\,\gamma_{\varphi\,j}{}^{i}\big)\,,\qquad\gamma_{\psi}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{\psi}&0\\
0&\penalty\ {\bar{\gamma}}_{\psi}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(30)

and for the
\beta
-functions from (
11
) and (
16
)

\displaystyle\beta_{y}{}^{i}={\tilde{\beta}}_{y}{}^{i}+\gamma_{\psi}\,y^{i}+y^{i}\,{\bar{\gamma}}_{\psi}+y^{j}\gamma_{\varphi\,j}{}^{i}\,,\quad\beta_{{\bar{y}}\hskip 0.5pti}={\tilde{\beta}}_{{\bar{y}}\hskip 0.5pti}+{\bar{\gamma}}_{\psi}\,{\bar{y}}_{i}+{\bar{y}}_{i}\,\gamma_{\psi}+\gamma_{\varphi\,i}{}^{j}\,{\bar{y}}_{j}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda\,ij}{}^{kl}={\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda\,ij}{}^{kl}+\gamma_{\varphi\,i}{}^{m}\lambda_{mj}{}^{kl}+\gamma_{\varphi\,i}{}^{m}\lambda_{im}{}^{kl}+\lambda_{j}{}^{ml}\,\gamma_{\varphi\,m}{}^{k}+\lambda_{j}{}^{km}\,\gamma_{\varphi\,m}{}^{l}\,,

(31)

where
\gamma_{\psi}\to{\bar{\gamma}}_{\psi}{\hskip 0.5pt}\ \beta_{y}{}^{i}\to\beta_{{\bar{y}}\hskip 0.5pti}
by taking

y^{i}\leftrightarrow{\bar{y}}_{i},\ \lambda_{ij}{}^{kl}\to\lambda_{kl}{}^{ij}
in each contribution.
For
{\bar{y}}_{i}=(y^{i})^{\dagger},\,\lambda_{\,kl}{}^{ij}=(\lambda_{\,ij}{}^{kl})^{*}
then
{\gamma}_{\psi}=\gamma_{\psi}{}^{\dagger}
and
\gamma_{\varphi\,j}{}^{i}=(\gamma_{\varphi\,i}{}^{j})^{*}
.
At one loop

\displaystyle\gamma_{\varphi\,i}{}^{j}{}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j})\,,\qquad\gamma_{\psi}{}^{(1)}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,,\qquad{\tilde{\beta}}_{y}{}^{i}{}^{(1)}=0\,,

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda\,ij}{}^{kl}{}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1a}\big(\lambda_{ij}{}^{mn}\lambda_{mn}{}^{kl}+2\,{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\,\lambda_{im}{}^{kn}\lambda_{jn}{}^{ml}\big)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1b}\,{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{l})\,,

(32)

where
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\,X_{ij}{}^{kl}=X_{ij}{}^{kl}+X_{ji}{}^{kl}=X_{ij}{}^{kl}+X_{ij}{}^{lk}
.
At two loops

\displaystyle\gamma_{\varphi\,i}{}^{j}{}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle 3\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}\,\lambda_{ik}{}^{mn}\lambda_{mn}{}^{kj}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}\,\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{j})+\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{k})\big)\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\psi}{}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{y}{}^{i}{}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}\,\lambda_{jk}{}^{li}\,y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{k}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}\,y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{k}\,,

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda\,ij}{}^{kl}{}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}\big(2\,\lambda_{ij}{}^{mn}\lambda_{mp}{}^{qk}\lambda_{nq}{}^{pl}+2\,\lambda_{ip}{}^{mq}\lambda_{jq}{}^{np}\lambda_{mn}{}^{kl}+{\mathcal{S}}_{4}\,\lambda_{ip}{}^{qk}\lambda_{jq}{}^{mn}\lambda_{mn}{}^{pl}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+2\,{\mathcal{S}}_{4}\,\lambda_{im}{}^{pn}\lambda_{jn}{}^{qk}\lambda_{pq}{}^{ml}\big)

\displaystyle{}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}\big(\lambda_{ij}{}^{mp}\lambda_{mq}{}^{kl}+{\mathcal{S}}_{4}\,\lambda_{im}{}^{kp}\lambda_{jq}{}^{lm}\big)\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{p}\,y^{q})

\displaystyle{}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\,{\mathcal{S}}_{4}\,\lambda_{im}{}^{kn}\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{l}{\bar{y}}_{n}\,y^{m})+\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{m}{\bar{y}}_{n}\,y^{l})\big)

\displaystyle{}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}\,\big(\lambda_{ij}{}^{mn}\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{m}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{n}\,y^{l})+\lambda_{mn}{}^{kl}\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{m}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{n})\big)

\displaystyle{}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}\,{\mathcal{S}}_{4}\,\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{m}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{l}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{m})+\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{m}\,y^{m}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{l})\big)

\displaystyle{}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2g}\,{\mathcal{S}}_{4}\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{m}\,y^{l}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{m})\,,

(33)

with
{\mathcal{S}}_{4}\,X_{ij}{}^{kl}=X_{ij}{}^{kl}+X_{ji}{}^{kl}+X_{ij}{}^{lk}+X_{ji}{}^{lk}
.

At three loops the results here reduce to 8 contributions for
\gamma_{\phi}

\displaystyle\gamma_{\varphi\,i}{}^{j}{}^{(3)}={}

\displaystyle{}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}\,\big(\lambda_{ik}{}^{mn}\lambda_{mn}{}^{lj}+2\,\lambda_{im}{}^{ln}\lambda_{kn}{}^{mj}\big)\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{k})

\displaystyle{}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}\,\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{j})+\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{k}\,)\big)\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{l})

\displaystyle{}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}\,\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{l}\,{\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{j})+\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{l}\,)\big)

\displaystyle{}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}\,\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{l}\,{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{j})+\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{l}{\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{k})\big)

\displaystyle{}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3h}\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{l}\,{\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{j})+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3m}\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{l}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{j})\,.

(34)

and 9 for

\displaystyle\gamma_{\psi}{}^{(3)}={}

\displaystyle 3\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}\,y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,\lambda_{im}{}^{kl}\lambda_{kl}{}^{mj}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}\,y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{l}\,\lambda_{ij}{}^{kl}

\displaystyle{}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}\,y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{k})\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{i})+\big(\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}\,y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{j}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{k}\big)\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{i})

For the scalar quartic
\beta
-function at three loops the 1PI contributions are restricted to 43 diagrams as

3n,\,3q,\,3\tilde{b},\,3\tilde{c},\,3\tilde{e},\,3\tilde{f},\,3\tilde{s},\,3\tilde{t},\,3\tilde{u},\,3\tilde{v},\,3\tilde{w},\,3\tilde{x},\,3\tilde{y}{\hskip 0.5pt},\,3\tilde{z},\,3e^{\prime},\,3f^{\prime},\,3g^{\prime},\,3i^{\prime},3j^{\prime}
are no longer present. There remain 7 primitive 3 loop diagrams.

There is one possible antisymmetric term at three loops

\upsilon_{\varphi\,i}{}^{j}{}^{(3)}=\upsilon_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}\,\big(\lambda_{ik}{}^{lm}\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{l}\,y^{k}{\bar{y}}_{m}\,y^{j})-\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{l}{\bar{y}}_{k}\,y^{m})\,\lambda_{lm}{}^{kj}\big)\,.

(37)

7
Supersymmetry Relations

Supersymmetry of course relates bosons and fermions. Imposing symmetry on the scalar
fermion theory leads to linear relations between the anomalous dimension and
\beta
-function
coefficients which we describe below.

7.1
{\mathcal{N}}=1
Supersymmetry

The Wess Zumino theory for scalars and fermions is a special case which can be obtained by restricting
the couplings of the theory with
U(1)
symmetry so that

y^{i}\to Y^{ijk}=Y^{(ijk)}\,,\qquad{\bar{y}}_{i}\to{\bar{Y}}_{ijk}={\bar{Y}}_{(ijk)}\,,\qquad\lambda_{ij}{}^{kl}\to{\bar{Y}}_{ijm}Y^{mkl}\,.

(38)

The usual non renormalisation theorems require

\gamma_{\psi}{}^{i}{}_{j}=\gamma_{\varphi}{}_{j}{}^{i}=\gamma^{i}{}_{j}\,,\qquad{\tilde{\beta}}_{Y}{\!}^{ijk}=0\,,\qquad{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda\,ij}{}^{kl}=2\,{\bar{Y}}_{ijm}\,\gamma^{m}{}_{n}Y^{nkl}\,.

(39)

At one loop this just imposes

\gamma^{S}_{1}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}=\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,\qquad 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1b}=0\,,\qquad\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1a}=2\,\gamma^{S}_{1}\,.

(40)

At two loops the necessary conditions are

\displaystyle\gamma^{S}_{2}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}=3\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}\,,\qquad\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}=0\,,

\displaystyle 2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}=0\,,\quad 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2g}=0\,,\quad\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}=0,\,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}=2\,\gamma^{S}_{2}\,.

(41)

At three loops the conditions on the anomalous dimensions and Yukawa couplings are then

\displaystyle\gamma^{S}_{3A}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}=\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}\,,\qquad\gamma^{S}_{3B}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}=\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3h}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma^{S}_{3C}=3\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}=2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma^{S}_{3D}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3p}=4\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3m}\,,

\displaystyle 4\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}+2\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3l}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3w}+2\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{z}}=0\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3s}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{o}}=0\,,\qquad\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}+2\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{p}}=0\,,

(42)

so that

\displaystyle\gamma^{S(1)}_{\phi}={}

(43)

with

\gamma^{S}_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,,\quad\gamma^{S}_{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,,\quad\gamma^{S}_{3A}=-\tfrac{1}{4}\,,\quad\gamma^{S}_{3B}=-\tfrac{1}{8}\,,\quad\gamma^{S}_{3C}=1\,,\quad\gamma^{S}_{3D}=\tfrac{3}{2}\zeta_{3}\,.

(44)

The contribution (
37
) to
\upsilon
vanishes on reduction to supersymmetry as in (
38
).

The constraints arising from (
39
) at three loops leads to
4 conditions relating
{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda}
to the anomalous dimensions

\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3h}=2\,\gamma^{S}_{3A}\,,\quad\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}=2\,\gamma^{S}_{3B}\,,\quad 3\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3p}=2\,\gamma^{S}_{3C}\,,\quad 2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3u}=2\,\gamma^{S}_{3D}\,,

(45)

and 14 linear homogeneous relations for
{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle 3\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3m}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3p}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{r}}=0\,,

\displaystyle 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3r}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{\imath}}=0\,,\quad\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3k}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{a}}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c^{\prime}}=0\,,

\displaystyle 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3t}+4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3v}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{h}}+\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{l}$}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3h^{\prime}}=0\,,\qquad

\displaystyle 2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3o}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3y}=0\,,\quad\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}+\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3h}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{o}}=0\,,\quad 2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{q}}=0\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{p}}=0\,,\quad 2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3v}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3z}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3d^{\prime}}=0\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}+\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{d}$}=0\,,\quad 8\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+8\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3w}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{n}}=0\,,\quad\quad 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3l}=0\,,

\displaystyle 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3s}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3x}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3a^{\prime}}=0\,,\quad

\displaystyle 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3r}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{g}}+2\,\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{k}$}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b^{\prime}}=0\,.

(46)

The last 5 relations involve contributions arising from non planar diagrams.

7.2
{\mathcal{N}}=\tfrac{1}{2}
Supersymmetry

This is a special case of the general scalar fermion theory where of course the number of real
scalars matches the number of fermions with
y^{a}\to Y^{abc}
a symmetric real tensor
and also
\lambda^{abcd}\to{\mathcal{S}}_{3}\,Y^{abe}Y^{cde}
. For this theory
\phi,\psi
can be
combined as a real superfield
\Phi
and in a perturbative expansion the diagrams
reduce to those of a simple
\phi^{3}
theory.
Under renormalisation as a four
dimensional theory there is a
\beta
-function
\beta_{Y}{}^{abc}

and anomalous dimension
\gamma_{\Phi}{}^{ab}
. For this case, unlike for
{\mathcal{N}}=1
supersymmetry,
there are non trivial
divergent vertex graphs so that
\beta_{Y}
and
\gamma_{\Phi}
are independent although the scalar

\beta
-function
\beta_{\lambda}
is determined in terms of these.

At lowest one loop order this gives

\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}=\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,\qquad\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}=2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1a}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}=4\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,\qquad 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1b}=0\,,

(47)

where

\gamma_{\Phi}{\!}^{(1)}=\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}\,\hbox to40.53pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-8.73582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

(48)

The equality
\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}
is a reflection of the choice
of normalisation of fermion traces in the main body of results. Each fermion trace gives the contribution of
a two component real fermion propagating round the loop.

At two loops equality of
\gamma_{\phi}{\hskip 0.5pt},\ \gamma_{\psi}
and symmetry of
\beta_{y}
requires

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt2A}=3\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt2B}=6\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2A}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\,,\quad\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2B}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}\,,\quad\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2C}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}\,,

(50)

Determining
\beta_{\lambda}
in terms of
\beta_{Y}
and
\gamma_{\phi}
imposes the restrictions

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}=2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt2A}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2A}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2B}\,,\qquad 0=\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt2B}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2C}\,,

\displaystyle 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}=4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}=-2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}=-2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}=-\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}=-\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2g}\,,\qquad\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}=0\,,

(51)

which implies further constraints on the Yukawa
\beta
-functions

\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}=-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}=2\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}\,,\qquad\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}=2(\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b})\,.

Reducing general results requires

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3A}={}

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3B}={}

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3h}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3C}={}

\displaystyle 2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}=3\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3D}={}

\displaystyle 2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+2\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3n}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3E}={}

\displaystyle 2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3o}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3F}={}

\displaystyle 2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}=6\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3h}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3l}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3G}={}

\displaystyle 12\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+4\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3l}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+2\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3m}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3H}={}

\displaystyle 4\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3k}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3k}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3I}={}

\displaystyle 4\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+2\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3m}=\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3p}\,.

JackU

Reducing the three loop scalar
\beta
-function to the
{\mathcal{N}}=\tfrac{1}{2}
theory requires large numbers
of relations. For the anomalous dimension and the symmetric
\beta

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3h}=2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3A}\,,\quad\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}=2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3B}\,,\quad 3\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3p}=2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3C}\,,\quad 0=\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3D}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}=2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3E}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3G}\,,\quad 6\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3q}=2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3F}\,,

\displaystyle 2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3t}=2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3H}\,,\quad 2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3u}=2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3I}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}=2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}=\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3h}=\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3z})=\tfrac{1}{2}(\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{a}})=\tfrac{1}{4}(2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{c}})

\displaystyle{}=\tfrac{1}{2}(6\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3q})=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3A}=\beta_{3B}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3D}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3G}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3I}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3K}\ \,,

\displaystyle 3\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3a}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3p}=\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}=\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}=2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3k}=2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3t}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{h}$}

\displaystyle{}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3C}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3E}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3F}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3H}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3J}\,,

\displaystyle 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3s}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3x}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3a^{\prime}}=0\,,

\displaystyle 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3r}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{g}}+2\,\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{k}$}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b^{\prime}}=0\,,

\displaystyle 4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3s}+\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{k}$}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3g^{\prime}}=0\,,

\displaystyle 8\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3c}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}+4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3u}+2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3i^{\prime}}=0\,,\qquad 2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3j^{\prime}}=0\,.

(64)

The 14 homogeneous relations in (
46
) are contained in (
63
), (
64
).
Combining (
63
), (
64
)
with (
62
) would apparently generate 51 conditions but 2 are redundant. Two of the conditions in
(

) imply
\beta_{Y{\hskip 0.5pt}3L}=\beta_{Y{\hskip 0.5pt}3Q}=0
and the relations in (
62
)

0=2\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{e}}
and

\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3b}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}+\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{d}$}=4\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}+\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}3l}
can be omitted since they are all zero in (
63
).
There remain 49 independent equations.

The conditions
\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3A}=\beta_{3B}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3D}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3G}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3I}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3K}
,

\beta_{3C}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3E}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3F}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3H}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3J}
,

\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3O}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3P}
and
\beta_{3L}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3M}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3Q}=0
impose
13 further relations on the Yukawa
\beta
-functions from (

).

8
Special Cases and Fixed Points

To analyse the RG flow in scalar fermion theories, and potentially find fixed points in an
\varepsilon=4-d
expansion,
it is generally necessary to restrict to cases where the RG flow is constrained to a small number of
couplings. Here we describe various examples where symmetries are imposed so that the RG flow is
reduced to two scalar couplings and one Yukawa coupling. Of course with minimal subtraction
\varepsilon
only
appears at zeroth order in a loop expansion so that various perturbative results listed here can easily be
used in the hunt for fixed points. Possible fixed points are first determined by using the
one loop contributions to the
\beta
-functions and are described in this section. Corresponding
two and three formulae which give
\varepsilon^{2},\varepsilon^{3}
contributions are
obtained by restriction of the general results and are presented in supplementary material.

At one loop the results obtained here for the general case give

\displaystyle{\beta}_{y}{\!}^{(1)a}={}

\displaystyle 2\,y^{b}y^{a}y^{b}+\tfrac{1}{2}\big(y^{b}y^{b}\,y^{a}+y^{a}\,y^{b}y^{b}\big)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{b}\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{b}y^{a})\,,

\displaystyle{\beta}_{\lambda}{\!}^{(1)abcd}={}

\displaystyle 3\,\lambda^{ef(ab}\lambda^{cd)ef}+2\,\lambda^{e(abc}\,\mathrm{tr}\big(y^{d)}y^{e}\big)-12\,\mathrm{tr}\big(y^{(a}y^{b}y^{c}y^{d)}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi}{\!}^{(1)ab}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(y^{a}y^{b})\,,\qquad\gamma_{\psi}{\!}^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{a}y^{a}\,,

(65)

with
\{y^{a}\}
symmetric and real.

For
n_{s}
real scalars and
n_{f}
pseudo real Majorana fermions the reduction to three couplings is achieved
by assuming

y^{a}\to y\,t^{a}\,{\mathds{1}}_{m}\,,\quad\lambda^{abcd}=\lambda\big(\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}+\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}\big)+g\,h^{abcd}\,,

(66)

with
\{t^{a}\}
a set of real traceless
n\times n
symmetric matrices and
m
essentially arbitrary.
We assume
3
3

3

The
corresponding scalar potential should be bounded below. The constraints on
\lambda,g

may be determined by the inequalities for any hermitian traceless
n\times n

t

k_{+}\mathrm{tr}(t^{2})^{2}\geq\mathrm{tr}(t^{4})\geq k_{-}\mathrm{tr}(t^{2})^{2}\,,\qquad k_{+}=\frac{(n-1)^{3}+1}{n^{2}(n-1)}\,,\quad k_{-}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n}\,,&n\ \mbox{even}\\
\frac{n^{2}+3}{n(n^{2}-1)}\,,&n\ \mbox{odd}\end{cases}\,.

For the potential to be bounded below it is possible for either
g
or
\lambda
to be negative
so long as
\lambda>0
,
3\,\lambda+k_{+}n\alpha\,g>0

or
g>0
,
3\,\lambda+k_{-}n\alpha\,g>0
.

\mathrm{tr}\big(t^{(a}\hskip 0.5ptt^{b}\hskip 0.5ptt^{c}\hskip 0.5ptt^{d)}\big)=n\,\alpha\,h^{abcd}\,.

(67)

In general the symmetry group is given by

R^{-1}y^{a}\,R={\tilde{R}}^{ab}y^{b}\quad\mbox{for}\quad R\in H_{f}\subset O(n_{f})\,,\ \ [{\tilde{R}}^{ab}]\in{H_{s}}\subset O(n_{s})\,.

(68)

and then
h^{abcd}
is an
H_{s}
invariant
O(n_{s})
symmetric tensor. Assuming (
66
)

H_{f}\simeq H\times O(m)
with
H\subset O(n)
. For simplicity we take
t^{a}
to be traceless and

H_{s}\simeq H/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
with
H
simple.

At one loop a consistent RG flow is achieved by
requiring for the Yukawa
\beta
-function the conditions

t^{a}{\hskip 0.5pt}t^{a}=n_{s}\,\alpha\,{\mathds{1}}_{n}\,,\qquad\mathrm{tr}(t^{a}{\hskip 0.5pt}t^{b})=n\,\alpha\,\delta^{ab}\,,\qquad t^{b}{\hskip 0.5pt}t^{a}{\hskip 0.5pt}t^{b}=\beta\,t^{a}\,,

(69)

where
n_{f}=n\,m
and
\alpha>0
depends on a choice of scale for
\{t^{a}\}
.
For the scalar coupling it is also necessary that

h^{ef(ab}h^{cd)ef}=A\big(\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}+\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}\big)+B\,h^{abcd}\,.

(70)

The tensor
h^{abcd}
may be further decomposed as

h^{abcd}=d^{abcd}+r\big(\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}+\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}\big)\,,\qquad r=\frac{2\,n_{s}\,\alpha+\beta}{3(n_{s}+2)}\,,

(71)

for
d^{abcd}
symmetric and traceless and (
66
) is alternatively expressed as

\lambda^{abcd}={\hat{\lambda}}\big(\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}+\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}\big)+g\,d^{abcd}\,,\qquad{\hat{\lambda}}=\lambda+r\,g\,.

(72)

With this definition (
70
) is equivalent to

d^{ef(ab}d^{cd)ef}=a\,\delta^{(ab}\delta^{cd)}+b\,d^{abcd}\,,

(73)

where
b=B-4\,r,\ a=3(A+rB)-(n_{s}+8)r^{2}
.

At higher loops the necessary constraints are such that the
\beta
-functions are reduced to
\beta_{y},\,\beta_{\lambda},\,\beta_{g}
and the anomalous dimension matrices have the form
\gamma_{\phi}\,\delta^{ab},\ \gamma_{\psi}\,{\mathds{1}}_{m}\times{\mathds{1}}_{n}
.

Using

h^{abcc}=\tfrac{1}{3}(2\,n_{s}\,\alpha+\beta)\,\delta^{ab}\,,

(74)

the lowest order results (
65
) are consistent with this form and give,

\displaystyle\beta_{y}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\,\beta+\tilde{n}_{f})y^{3}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle(n_{s}+8)\lambda^{2}+\tfrac{2}{3}(2\,n_{s}\,\alpha+\beta)\lambda\,g+4\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,\lambda\,y^{2}+3\,A\,g^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{g}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle 12\,\lambda\,g+3\,B\,g^{2}+4\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,g\,y^{2}-24\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{4}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{2}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\psi}{\!}^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,n_{s}\,\alpha\,y^{2}\,,\qquad\tilde{n}_{f}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha\,n_{f}\,.

(75)

Alternatively

\displaystyle{\hat{\lambda}}=\lambda+r\,g\,,\quad\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hat{\lambda}}$}{}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle(n_{s}+8){\hat{\lambda}}^{2}+a\,g^{2}+4\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,y^{2}-24\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,r\,y^{4}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{g}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle 12\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,g+3\,b\,g^{2}+4\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,g\,y^{2}-24\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{4}\,.

(76)

For quadratic scalar operators then at lowest order the anomalous dimension for the singlet
\sigma=\phi^{2}

and the corresponding matrix for
\rho^{ab}=\phi^{a}\phi^{b}-\frac{1}{n_{s}}\delta^{ab}\phi^{2}
are just

\displaystyle\gamma_{\sigma}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle(n_{s}+2){\hat{\lambda}}+2\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\rho}{\!}^{(1)ab,cd}={}

\displaystyle 2({\hat{\lambda}}+\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{2})\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}-\tfrac{1}{n_{s}}\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}\big)+g\,d^{abcd}\,.

(77)

At higher orders, besides the symmetric traceless tensor
d^{abcd}
, it is necessary to take into account the
mixed symmetry tensor
w^{abcd}
defined by

2\,\mathrm{tr}\big(t^{(a}t^{b)}t^{(c}t^{d)}\big)-\mathrm{tr}\big(t^{a}t^{c}t^{b}t^{d}\big)-\mathrm{tr}\big(t^{a}t^{d}t^{b}t^{c}\big)=n\,\alpha\big(w^{abcd}-s(\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}-2\,\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd})\big)\,,

(78)

which with

s=\frac{n_{s}\,\alpha-\beta}{n_{s}-1}\,,

(79)

satisfies
w^{abcd}=w^{(ab)(cd)}=w^{cdab},\,w^{a(bcd)}=0
and is traceless on contraction of any pair of indices.
This contributes to
\gamma_{\rho}{\!}^{ab,cd}
at two and higher loops. The anomalous dimensions
are then dictated by the eigenvalues of
d^{abcd}
and
w^{abcd}
as
\frac{1}{2}(n-1)(n+2)\times\frac{1}{2}(n-1)(n+2)
symmetric matrices. There are discussed in appendix
B
. In general
there are three eigenvalues as symmetric traceless tensors decompose into components belonging
to representation spaces of the reduced symmetry group
H
.

If
a=0
then in (
71
)
d^{abcd}=0
and the
g
coupling is redundant. The scalar
\beta
-function
at one loop is given just by
\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hat{\lambda}}$}{}^{(1)}
in (
129
) with
g=0
.
This restriction necessarily holds for
n=2,3
since, for any traceless
t
,

\mathrm{tr}(t^{4})=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(t^{2})^{2}
. This translates into the condition
\frac{1}{2}(n_{s}+2)n\,\alpha=2\,n_{s}\,\alpha+\beta
.
In this case
H=O(n_{s})
and there are just two anomalous dimensions for quadratic scalars
\gamma_{\sigma},\,\gamma_{\rho}
with

\gamma_{\sigma}{\!}^{(1)}=(n_{s}+2){\hat{\lambda}}+2\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{2}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\rho}{\!}^{(1)}=2({\hat{\lambda}}+\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{2})\,.

(80)

Two extreme examples of matrices satisfying (
69
) are given by

1, Symmetric.
t^{a}\to s^{a}
where
\{s^{a}\}
are a basis for
symmetric traceless
n\times n
real matrices with
n\geq 2
satisfying the completeness condition

(s^{a})_{\alpha\beta}(s^{a})_{\gamma\delta}=\delta_{\alpha\gamma}\,\delta_{\beta\delta}+\delta_{\alpha\delta}\,\delta_{\beta\gamma}-\frac{2}{n}\,\delta_{\alpha\beta}\,\delta_{\gamma\delta}
, and
\mathrm{tr}(s^{a}s^{b})=2\,\delta^{ab}
.
\{s^{a}\}
are the generators
corresponding to the coset
Sl(n,{\mathbb{R}})/SO(n)
.

2, Diagonal.
t^{a}
are traceless diagonal
n\times n
real matrices with
n\geq 2
. A basis is
obtained by taking
(t^{a})_{\alpha\beta}=e^{a}_{\alpha}\,\delta_{\alpha\beta}
where
e^{a}_{\alpha},\ \alpha=1,\dots,n

form the
n
vertices of a
n-1
dimensional hypertetrahedron and satisfy
\sum_{\alpha}e^{a}_{\alpha}=0,\,\sum_{\alpha}e^{a}_{\alpha}e^{b}_{\alpha}=2\,\delta^{ab}
with
e^{a}_{\alpha}e^{a}_{\beta}=2\,\delta_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{2}{n}
.
In this example the tensor
w^{abcd}
in (
78
) vanishes.

For these cases we have

For purely scalar theories these examples were described long ago in

In general defining

\displaystyle S_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}={}

\displaystyle(t^{a})_{(\alpha\beta}(t^{a})_{\gamma\delta)}-\tfrac{2\,n_{s}\,\alpha}{3(n+2)}\big(\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{\gamma\delta}+\delta_{\alpha\gamma}\delta_{\beta\delta}+\delta_{\alpha\delta}\delta_{\beta\gamma}\big)\,,

\displaystyle W_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}={}

\displaystyle(t^{a})_{\alpha\beta}(t^{a})_{\gamma\delta}-\tfrac{1}{2}\big((t^{a})_{\alpha\gamma}(t^{a})_{\beta\delta}+(t^{a})_{\alpha\delta}(t^{a})_{\beta\gamma}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt{}+\tfrac{n_{s}\,\alpha}{n-1}\big(\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{\gamma\delta}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\delta_{\alpha\gamma}\delta_{\beta\delta}+\delta_{\alpha\delta}\delta_{\beta\gamma})\big)\,,

(84)

then positivity of
S_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}S_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
and

W_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}W_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
give the bounds

\big(\tfrac{2\,n_{s}}{n+2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,n\big)\alpha\leq\beta\leq\big(n-\tfrac{n_{s}}{n-1}\big)\alpha\,,

(85)

which entails
n_{s}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(n-1)(n+2)
. This is of course saturated in case 1 and the upper bound on
\beta

is saturated in case 2.
With
O^{ab}=t^{a}\,t^{b}-\tfrac{1}{n_{s}}\,\delta^{ab}\,t^{c}t^{c}
then since
|\mathrm{tr}(O^{ab}\,O^{ab})|\leq\mathrm{tr}(O^{ba}\,O^{ab})
we must have also the bounds

-(n_{s}-2)\alpha\leq\beta\leq n_{s}\,\alpha\,.

(86)

General results for fermion scalar theories can be restricted to
n_{s}
real scalars and
n_{f}
Dirac fermions
by taking

y^{a}\to\begin{pmatrix}0&y\,t^{a}\\
{\bar{y}}\,{\bar{t}}^{a}&0\end{pmatrix}{\mathds{1}}_{m}\,,\qquad{\bar{t}}^{a}=(t^{a})^{\dagger}\,.

(87)

with
\{t^{a}\}

n\times n
matrices so that

. Assuming the Yukawa interaction satisfies

U^{-1}y^{a}\,U={\tilde{R}}^{ab}y^{b}\quad\mbox{for}\quad U\in H_{f}\subset U(n_{f})\times U(n_{f})\,,\ \ [{\tilde{R}}^{ab}]\in{H_{s}}\subset O(n_{s})\,,

)

Here we require as previously that
\{t^{a}\}
are

.

For each fermion trace the reduction of general results is obtained by taking

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(y^{a_{1}}y^{a_{2}}\dots y^{a_{2p}})\to{}

\displaystyle m\,(y{\bar{y}})^{n}\big(\mathrm{tr}(t^{a_{1}}{\bar{t}}^{a_{2}}\dots{\bar{t}}^{a_{2p}})+\mathrm{tr}({\bar{t}}^{a_{1}}t^{a_{2}}\dots t^{a_{2p}})

\displaystyle{}\hskip 45.52458pt+\mathrm{tr}({\bar{t}}^{a_{2p}}\dots{\bar{t}}^{a_{2}}t^{a_{1}})+\mathrm{tr}(t^{a_{2p}}\dots t^{a_{2}}{\bar{t}}^{a_{1}})\big)\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(y^{a_{1}}y^{a_{2}}\dots y^{a_{2p+1}})\to{}

\displaystyle 0\,.

The identities in (

t^{a}{\hskip 0.5pt}{\bar{t}}^{\hskip 0.8pta}=n_{s}\,\alpha\,{\mathds{1}}_{n}\,,\quad\mathrm{tr}(t^{a}{\hskip 0.5pt}{\bar{t}}{\hskip 0.8pt}^{b})=n\,\alpha\,\delta^{ab}\,,\quad t^{b}{\hskip 0.5pt}{\bar{t}}{\hskip 0.5pt}^{a}{\hskip 0.5pt}t^{b}=\beta\,t^{a}\,,\quad\mathrm{tr}\big(t^{\hskip 0.5pt(a}{\bar{t}}^{\hskip 0.8ptb}t^{\hskip 0.5ptc}{\bar{t}^{\hskip 0.8ptd)}}\big)=n\,\alpha\,h^{abcd}\,.

(110)

More general versions of these equations with more than one
d
-tensor were discussed for various
n
in
Liendo
.

For the particular cases considered here

\alpha_{d}

\beta_{d}

\gamma_{d}

1.

\tfrac{1}{2n}\,(n-2)(n+4)

\tfrac{1}{4n}(n^{2}+4n-24)

\tfrac{1}{8n^{2}}(n-4)(3n^{2}+4n-80)

2.

\tfrac{2}{n}\,(n-2)

where
(n_{s}+2)A_{d}+\tfrac{1}{3}(2n_{s}\,\alpha+\beta)B_{d}=\tfrac{1}{3}(2\alpha_{d}+\beta_{d})\alpha_{d}
.
For the different cases we have

\displaystyle\text{
\begin{tabular}[]{ c c c c c}&$A_{d}$&$B_{d}$&$\beta_{1}$\\
\vskip 3.0pt\cr\hline\cr\vskip 4.0pt\cr 1.&$\tfrac{1}{12n^{2}}(3n^{2}+32)$&$\tfrac{1}{8n}(n^{2}+8n-64)$&$\tfrac{1}{2}(5n^{2}+14n-72)$\\
\vskip 4.0pt\cr 2.&$\tfrac{8}{3n^{2}}$&$\tfrac{2}{n}(n-4)$&$2(7n-18)$\\
\vskip 4.0pt\cr 3.&$\tfrac{1}{6n^{2}}\,(3n^{2}+16)$&$\tfrac{1}{4n}(n^{2}-32)$&$5n^{2}-36$\\
\vskip 4.0pt\cr 6.&$\tfrac{1}{12n^{2}}(3n^{2}+32)$&$\tfrac{1}{8n}(n^{2}-8n-64)$&$\tfrac{1}{2}(5n^{2}-14n-72)$\end{tabular}
}\ .

The tensors
d^{abc}
defined as in (
107
) may be used to form symmetric traceless
Yukawa couplings so that the number of fermions
n_{f}=m\,n_{s}
. In this case (
75
) becomes

\displaystyle\beta_{y}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle(\alpha_{d}+2\,\beta_{d}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha_{d}\,m)y^{3}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle(n_{s}+8)\lambda^{2}+\tfrac{2}{3}(2\,n_{s}\,\alpha+\beta)\lambda\,g+2\,\alpha_{d}\,m\,\lambda\,y^{2}+3\,A\,g^{2}-12\,A_{d}\,m\,y^{4}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{g}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle 12\,\lambda\,g+3\,B\,g^{2}+2\,\alpha_{d}\,m\,g\,y^{2}-12\,B_{d}\,m\,y^{4}

\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha_{d}\,m\,y^{2}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\psi}{\!}^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha_{d}\,y^{2}\,.

(129)

As was discussed in detail in
Emergent
for
m=1,\,n_{s}=n_{f}
there is a reduction to a single component

{\mathcal{N}}=\frac{1}{2}
supersymmetric theory with the couplings constrained by

\lambda=\tfrac{2}{n}\,y^{2}\,,\qquad g=3\,y^{2}\,.

(130)

The results in (
129
) with this restriction all correspond to

\beta_{y^{2}}{\!}^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{n}\,\beta_{1}\,y^{4}\,,

(131)

with formulae for
\beta_{1}
given in (
8.1
). The reduction depends on non trivial relations between

A,A_{d},B,B_{d}
which are satisfied in each of the cases listed. Except for the last case
\beta_{1}>0
, and
there are fixed points in an
\varepsilon
-expansion, for
n\geq 3
.
For the
{\mathcal{N}}=\frac{1}{2}
supersymmetric theory then taking
Y^{abc}\to y\,d^{abc}

there is a single coupling theory with a
\beta
-function and
\gamma_{\Phi}
, with three loop coefficients given in
(
60
), (
61
), expanded as

\displaystyle\beta_{y}={}

\displaystyle\big(\tfrac{3}{2}\,\alpha_{d}+2\,\beta_{d}\big)y^{3}-3\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha_{d}{\!}^{2}+\alpha_{d}\,\beta_{d}+2\,\beta_{d}{\!}^{2}\big)y^{5}

\displaystyle+\big(\tfrac{15}{8}\,\alpha_{d}{\!}^{3}+\tfrac{9}{4}\,\alpha_{d}{\!}^{2}\beta_{d}+\tfrac{27}{2}\,\alpha_{d}\,\beta_{d}{\!}^{2}+30\,\beta_{d}{\!}^{3}\big)y^{7}

\displaystyle{}+\big((\tfrac{9}{2}\,\alpha_{d}+30\,\beta_{d})\gamma_{d}+36\,\epsilon_{d}\big)\zeta_{3}\,y^{7}+{\rm O}(y^{9})\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha_{d}\,y^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\alpha_{d}{\!}^{2}y^{4}+\big(\tfrac{5}{8}\,\alpha_{d}{\!}^{3}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\alpha_{d}{\!}^{2}\beta_{d}+\tfrac{5}{2}\,\alpha_{d}\,\beta_{d}{\!}^{2}+\tfrac{3}{2}\,\alpha_{d}\,\gamma_{d}\,\zeta_{3}\big)y^{6}+{\rm O}(y^{8})\,.

(132)

Of course the lowest order term corresponds to (
129
). Furthermore

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Phi^{2}}={}

\displaystyle 2\,\alpha_{d}\,y^{2}-(5\,\alpha_{d}{\!}^{2}+4\,\alpha_{d}\beta_{d})y^{4}

\displaystyle{}+\big(10\,\alpha_{d}{\!}^{3}+7\,\alpha_{d}{\!}^{2}\beta_{d}+22\,\alpha_{d}\,\beta_{d}{\!}^{2}+6(\alpha_{d}{\!}^{2}\beta_{d}+6\,\alpha_{d}\,\beta_{d}{\!}^{2}+4\,\alpha_{d}\,\gamma_{d})\zeta_{3}\big)y^{6}

\displaystyle{}+2\,\gamma_{\Phi}+{\rm O}(y^{8})\,.

(133)

The bounds (
85
) becomes
-\frac{n_{s}-2}{2(n_{s}+2)}\,\alpha_{d}\leq\beta_{d}\leq\tfrac{n_{s}-2}{n_{s}-1}\,\alpha_{d}
. The upper bound for
\beta_{d}
corresponds to the vanishing of
W_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
in (
84
) and holds exactly for case 2 in (
116
) for any
n
.
The lower bound for
\beta_{d}
corresponds to the vanishing of
S_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
in (
84
) which becomes in this case the condition

d^{abe}d^{cde}+d^{ade}d^{bce}+d^{ace}d^{dbe}=K\big(\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}+\delta^{ac}\delta^{db}\big)\,,\quad K=\tfrac{2\,\alpha_{d}}{n_{s}+2}\,,

}\Big)

(135)

This was analysed in
birdtracks
and related to the
F_{4}
family of Lie groups.

A uniform treatment is obtained by considering hermitian traceless
n\times n
matrices
\{e_{a}\}
,

a=1,\dots,n_{s}
, satisfying

\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{a}e_{b}+e_{b}e_{a})=\tfrac{1}{n}\,\delta_{ab}\,\mathds{1}_{n}+d_{abc}\,e_{c}\,,\qquad d_{abc}=d\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle(abc)$}\,,\ \ d_{aac}=0\,.

(136)

Of course this implies
e_{a}e_{a}=\tfrac{1}{n}n_{s}\,\mathds{1}_{n},\ \mathrm{tr}(e_{a}e_{b})=\delta_{ab}
.
The algebra defined by (
136
) is equivalent to the result that hermitian real, complex and quaternionic
matrices form a special real Jordan algebra. Furthermore
3\times 3
hermitian octonionic matrices also
form an exceptional real Jordan, or Albert, algebra, with
F_{4}
as the automorphism group.
For
3\times 3
hermitian traceless matrices

x=x_{a}e_{a}\,,\qquad x^{3}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{tr}(x^{2})\,x-\tfrac{1}{3}\,\mathrm{tr}(x^{3})\,\mathds{1}_{3}=0\,.

(137)

This is just the Cayley Hamilton theorem for real or complex matrices in these cases.
The result also extends to hermitian traceless quaternionic and even octonionic matrices
Octonion
where
the trace is just the sum of the real diagonal elements. (
137
) is
equivalent to (
134
) with just
K=\frac{1}{6}
. For each case it is straightforward to check

\displaystyle\text{
\begin{tabular}[]{ c c c c c }&${\mathbb{R}}$&${\mathbb{C}}$&${\mathbb{H}}$&${\mathbb{O}}$\\
\vskip 3.0pt\cr\hline\cr\vskip 4.0pt\cr$n_{s}$&5&8&14&26\end{tabular}
}\ \ .

Defining
{\tilde{\alpha}}_{d},\,{\tilde{\beta}}_{d},\,{\tilde{\gamma}}_{d},\,{\tilde{\delta}}_{d},\,{\tilde{\epsilon}}_{d}

just as in (
109
) and (
110
) with normalisation dictated by (
136
) then applying (
135
)
to each diagram gives the relations

\displaystyle 2\,{\tilde{\beta}}_{d}+{\tilde{\alpha}}_{d}=\tfrac{1}{3}\,,\quad{\tilde{\gamma}}_{d}+2\,{\tilde{\beta}}_{d}{}^{2}=\tfrac{1}{6}\big({\tilde{\beta}}_{d}+2\,{\tilde{\alpha}}_{d}\big)\,,\quad 2\,{\tilde{\epsilon}}_{d}+{\tilde{\beta}}_{d}\,{\tilde{\gamma}}_{d}=\tfrac{1}{6}\,{\tilde{\beta}}_{d}\big(2\,{\tilde{\beta}}_{d}+{\tilde{\alpha}}_{d}\big)\,,

\displaystyle{\tilde{\delta}}_{d}+{\tilde{\epsilon}}_{d}+{\tilde{\beta}}_{d}{}^{3}=\tfrac{1}{6}\big({\tilde{\gamma}}_{d}+{\tilde{\beta}}_{d}{}^{2}+{\tilde{\alpha}}_{d}{}^{2}\big)\,.

(140)

Since (
134
) directly determines
{\tilde{\alpha}}_{d}
the results are then

{\tilde{\alpha}}_{d}

{\tilde{\beta}}_{d}

{\tilde{\gamma}}_{d}

{\tilde{\delta}}_{d}

{\tilde{\epsilon}}_{d}

\tfrac{1}{12}({\scriptstyle n_{s}+2})

-\tfrac{1}{24}({\scriptstyle n_{s}-2})

-\tfrac{1}{2^{5}.3^{2}}({\scriptstyle n_{s}{\!}^{2}-10\,n_{s}-16})

\tfrac{1}{2^{8}.3^{3}}(\scriptstyle{n_{s}{\!}^{3}-3\,n_{s}{\!}^{2}+80\,n_{s}+100}
)

-\tfrac{1}{2^{9}.3^{3}}{\scriptstyle n_{s}}({\scriptstyle n_{s}-2})({\scriptstyle n_{s}-10})

(141)

For
n_{s}=5,8,14
these results are identical to the corresponding results obtained above apart from a change of normalisation.
That the only solutions of (
134
) are given by (
8.1
) was demonstrated in
birdtracks
as a
consequence of various bounds following from (
134
).

8.2
Fixed Points

Extending to
4-\varepsilon
dimensions the interactions become marginally relevant and the
\beta
-functions
in a
\overline{M\!S}
scheme take the form

{\hat{\beta}}_{y}{\!}^{a}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon\,y^{a}+{\beta}_{y}{\!}^{a}\,,\qquad{\hat{\beta}}_{\lambda}{\!}^{abcd}=-\varepsilon\,\lambda^{abcd}+{\beta}_{\lambda}{\!}^{abcd}\,.

(142)

There are then fixed points which can be analysed in terms of an
\varepsilon
expansion.

In the restricted theories described previously we consider first the case where
there are just two couplings
y,\lambda
. Within the examples discussed here this
coresponds to requiring
a=0
as given in (
) where the various possiblities
arise for
n_{s}=1,2,3,5,8,14
.
At lowest order from (
75
) and (
129
), with
\tilde{n}_{f}=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha\,n_{f}
from (
75
),

\displaystyle y_{*}{\!}^{2}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,Y\,\varepsilon\,,\qquad\lambda_{*\pm}=\frac{1}{2(n_{s}+8)}\big(1-2\,Y\,\tilde{n}_{f}\pm\sqrt{Z}\,\big)\varepsilon\,,

(143)

for

Y=\frac{1}{n_{s}\,\alpha+2\,\beta+\tilde{n}_{f}}\,,\quad Z=1+\frac{12}{n_{s}+2}\,\big(n_{s}(n_{s}+10)\alpha+4\,\beta\big)\,Y^{2}\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,.

(144)

For
n_{f}
Dirac fermions the results for fixed points remain unchanged except
y^{2}\to y{\bar{y}}
and it is necessary to take
in (
143
) and (
144
)
n_{f}\to 4n_{f}
.
For
Z=0
there is a bifurcation point. This requires
\beta
to be sufficiently negative but
with just the lower bound in (
86
) this is impossible and for non zero
n_{f}

Z>1

so that
\lambda_{*-}<0
which leads to an unstable scalar potential.
For
n_{f}=0
the fixed points are just
\lambda_{*+}=\varepsilon/(n_{s}+8),\ \lambda_{*-}=0
which reproduce the fixed points for the purely scalar
O(n_{s})
theory.
From (
143
) and (
144
) to leading order for large
\tilde{n}_{f}

y_{*}{\!}^{2}\sim\frac{1}{2\,\tilde{n}_{f}}\,\varepsilon\,,\qquad\lambda_{*+}\sim 6\,r\,\frac{1}{\tilde{n}_{f}}\,\varepsilon\,,\qquad\lambda_{*-}\sim-\frac{1}{n_{s}+8}\,\varepsilon\,.

(145)

Since
\lambda_{*-}<0
this case is seemingly not relevant.

The anomalous dimensions at the fixed point to lowest order are then just

\gamma_{\phi}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,Y\,\varepsilon\,,\qquad\gamma_{\psi}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,n_{s}\,\alpha\,Y\,\varepsilon\,.

(146)

For just two couplings the stability matrix at a fixed point becomes

M=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\lambda}{\hat{\beta}}_{\lambda}&\partial_{\lambda}\beta_{y}\\
\partial_{y}\beta_{\lambda}&\partial_{y}{\hat{\beta}}_{y}\end{pmatrix}\bigg|_{\lambda=\lambda_{*},y=y_{*}}\,.

(147)

At lowest order since
\partial_{\lambda}\beta_{y}=0

the eigenvalues obtained from (
65
) and (
75
) for the fixed points corresponding to

\lambda_{*\pm}
are then given by

\kappa_{\pm}=\big(1,\pm\sqrt{Z}\,\big)\varepsilon\,.

(148)

The theories described in (
) and (
) reduce to the case where there
are just two couplings
\lambda,\,y
when
n=2,3
. Theories corresponding to
n=2
are well known.
For case 2 in (

to the Gross Neveu model, a fermionic generalisation of the Ising model. For case 1 in (

n_{s}=2

when
n=2
and this is a renormalisable form of Nambu Jona-Lasinio model which has complex scalars
and extends the
XY
model.
Case 3 and case 5 are identical for
n=2
, reflecting
SU(2)\simeq Sp(2)
and this extends the Heisenberg theory
for
n_{s}=3
. Examples corresponding to taking
n=3
do not seem to have been considered previously.

The results
in
Zerf2
correspond to those described here by taking
\lambda\to\lambda/4,\,y\to 2y^{2},\,n\to\frac{1}{2}\tilde{n}_{f}

and
\gamma_{\phi}/2\to\gamma_{\phi},\,\gamma_{\psi}/2\to\gamma_{\psi},\,\beta_{y}/4\to y\beta_{y},\,4\,\beta_{\lambda}\to\beta_{\lambda}
. The results in
