Results in
Zerf2
extend to four loops.
There is an extensive literature considering large
n_{f}

Fei2
.
For the
XY
theory and
\tilde{n}_{f}=1
then, as is well known
Thomas
;
Grover:2013rc
;
Fei:2016sgs
,
there is a supersymmetric fixed point with, at lowest order

\lambda_{*+}=y_{*}{\!}^{2}=\frac{1}{6}\,\varepsilon
,
\gamma_{\phi*}=\gamma_{\psi*}=\frac{1}{6}\,\varepsilon
,

\gamma_{\sigma*}=\varepsilon,\ \gamma_{\rho*}=\frac{2}{3}\,\varepsilon
and stability matrix eigenvalues
(1,3)\varepsilon
.
For the Ising case and
\tilde{n}_{f}=\tfrac{1}{2}

there is an apparent
{\mathcal{N}}=1
supersymmetric fixed point
Thomas
;
Grover:2013rc
;
Fei:2016sgs
;
Gies

with

\lambda_{*+}=y_{*}{\!}^{2}=\frac{1}{7}\,\varepsilon
,
\gamma_{\phi*}=\gamma_{\psi*}=\frac{1}{14}\,\varepsilon
,

\gamma_{\sigma*}=\tfrac{4}{7}\,\varepsilon
and stability matrix eigenvalues
(1,\frac{13}{7})\varepsilon
.

For three non zero couplings results are more involved. At lowest order from (
75
)
fixed points are determined by solving

\displaystyle 0={}

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon\,y+(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\,\beta+\tilde{n}_{f})y^{3}\,,

\displaystyle 0={}

\displaystyle\big({-\varepsilon}+4\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{2}\big)\lambda+(n_{s}+8)\lambda^{2}+\tfrac{2}{3}(2\,n_{s}\,\alpha+\beta)\lambda\,g+3\,A\,g^{2}\,,

\displaystyle 0={}

\displaystyle\big({-\varepsilon}+4\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{2}\big)g+12\,\lambda\,g+3\,B\,g^{2}-24\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,y^{4}\,,

(159)

for the various choices of
\alpha,\,\beta
and
n_{s}
.
The example of hermitian
y^{a}
was considered in
Ji
though our results differ in one term in
\beta_{\lambda}
.
A large
N
analysis for Yukawa couplings given in terms of Lie algebra generators was discussed in

GraceyGN
. For the purely scalar theories obtained
when
n_{f}=0
there are no fixed points with both
\lambda,\,g
non zero for the theories discussed here
for allowed
n
except in case 4 when
n=4
and in case 2 for arbitrary
n
. This latter case corresponds to the hypertetrahedral theory discussed in
ZiaW
and more recently in
Seeking
. Besides the Gaussian
fixed point with vanishing couplings there is of course always
the Heisenberg fixed point with, at lowest order,

\lambda_{*H}=\frac{1}{n_{s}+8}\,\varepsilon\,,\quad g_{H*}=0\,,\quad\big(\kappa_{1H},\kappa_{2H}\big)=\Big(1,\,\tfrac{4-n_{s}}{n_{s}+8}\Big)\varepsilon\,,

(160)

which clearly becomes unstable, in this approximation when
n_{s}>4
.

For a non zero Yukawa coupling it is trivial to solve (
159
) to determine
y_{*}{\!}^{2}
to

{\rm O}(\varepsilon)
. Furthermore
\lambda
appears only linearly in the
\beta_{g}
equation
so that the fixed point equations reduce to just finding the roots of a quartic polynomial
f(g)
.
In consequence there are generically four possible roots though of course these may be complex.
Once
y_{*}{\!}^{2}
is eliminated the equations (
159
) have a crucial symmetry under

\tilde{n}_{f}\to\frac{(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\,\beta)^{2}}{\tilde{n}_{f}}\,,\qquad\varepsilon\to-\varepsilon\,.

(161)

This relates solutions for large and small
\tilde{n}_{f}
. Under this transformation it is easy to verify from
(
143
) and (
144
) that, since
Y\to\tilde{n}_{f}Y/(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\beta)
,

\lambda_{*+}\leftrightarrow\lambda_{*-}
.

When
\tilde{n}_{f}
is small the Yukawa interaction is weakly relevant. The Gaussian fixed point is perturbed
to give

g_{*}\sim-\frac{6}{(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\beta)^{2}}\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,\varepsilon+\frac{108\,B}{(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\beta)^{4}}\,\tilde{n}_{f}{\!}^{2}\,\varepsilon\,,\qquad\lambda_{*}\sim\frac{108\,A}{(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\beta)^{4}}\,\tilde{n}_{f}{\!}^{2}\,\varepsilon\,,

(162)

and starting from the
O(n_{s})
symmetric fixed point

g_{*}\sim\frac{6(n_{s}+8)}{(n_{s}-4)(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\beta)^{2}}\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,\varepsilon\,,\quad\lambda_{*}\sim\frac{1}{n_{s}+8}\,\varepsilon+\frac{6\big(n_{s}(n_{s}+12)\alpha+8\beta\big)}{(n_{s}-4)(n_{s}+8)(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\beta)^{4}}\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,\varepsilon\,.

(163)

If there are additional fixed point solutions for
\tilde{n}_{f}=0
, as in the special cases described above, these disappear
for very tiny non zero
\tilde{n}_{f}
.

When
\tilde{n}_{f}
is large a corresponding pattern related by (
161
) emerges.
For
\lambda,\,g
both of
{\rm O}(1)
as
n_{f}\to\infty
, then since
4\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,y_{*}{\!}^{2}\to 2\varepsilon
,
it is easy to see that the fixed point equations are, to leading order, of
the same form as the purely scalar theory but with
\varepsilon\to-\varepsilon
. For
\varepsilon>0
this
leads in general to a scalar potential which is not bounded below and in any event there are no
solutions except in the cases described above. For large
n_{f}
there are solutions with both

g,\,\lambda
small which have the form

y_{*}{\!}^{2}\sim\frac{1}{2\,\tilde{n}_{f}}\,\varepsilon\,,\qquad g_{*}\sim 6\,\frac{1}{\tilde{n}_{f}}\,\varepsilon-108\,B\,\frac{1}{\tilde{n}_{f}{\!}^{2}}\,\varepsilon\,,\qquad\lambda_{*}\sim-108\,A\,\frac{1}{\tilde{n}_{f}{\!}^{2}}\,\varepsilon\,,

(164)

where the scalar potential is bounded below, and also for
\lambda={\rm O}(1)

g_{*}\sim 6\,\frac{n_{s}+8}{n_{s}-4}\frac{1}{\tilde{n}_{f}}\,\varepsilon\,,\qquad\lambda_{*}\sim-\frac{1}{n_{s}+8}\,\varepsilon\,.

(165)

The second solution leads to instabilities so only (
164
) remains as a valid possibility.

For intermediate
\tilde{n}_{f}
the possible fixed points depend on
n
.
For lowish
n\lesssim 7
the number of solutions drops to zero as
\tilde{n}_{f}
increases and
then goes back to two (for case 2 this happens if
n<5
and for case 6 if
n\leq 14)
.
For higher
n
the number of solutions jumps from 2 to 4 with
increasing
\tilde{n}_{f}
and then reverts to two which match on to (
164
) and (
165
) for large

\tilde{n}_{f}
(for case 2 and case 4 if
n=4
there are four solutions for very large
\tilde{n}_{f}
and very tiny
\tilde{n}_{f}

as the purely scalar theories have fixed point in these cases). The critical
n
dividing the two cases
is determined by
d(n_{c})=0
for
d(n)=\tfrac{4}{9}(2n_{s}\alpha+\beta)^{2}-12(n_{s}+8)A
. For
d(n)<0
as happens
for
n<n_{c}
then
h(\lambda,g)=(n_{s}+8)\lambda^{2}+\tfrac{2}{3}(2n_{s}\alpha+\beta)\lambda g+3A\,g^{2}>0
. For

4\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,y_{*}{\!}^{2}=\varepsilon
, or
n_{f}=n_{s}\alpha+2\beta
, the lowest order fixed point equations require

h(\lambda_{*},g_{*})=0
. This ensures that there can be no fixed point solutions for a finite region

n\lesssim n_{c}
. Conversely for
n\gtrsim n_{c}

h(\lambda,g)
is no longer positive definite and there are solutions
with
g
non zero.
In consequence for
n\approx n_{c}
and
\tilde{n}_{f}\approx n_{f,c}=(n_{s}\alpha+2\beta)|_{n=n_{c}}

there are either
0
or
4
solutions
For the symmetric case
n_{c}=6
and
\tilde{n}_{f,c}=8
.
5
5

5

For
n=6,\,\tilde{n}_{f}=8
the
fixed point solutions become
y_{*}{\!}^{2}=\frac{1}{32}\varepsilon,\ \lambda_{*}=\pm\frac{1}{24}\varepsilon,\ g_{*}=\mp\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon
. In neither case are the conditions for
a stable potential satisfied.
For the other cases the results for
(n_{c},\,n_{f,c})
are then
2.
(4.37,2.31)
, 3.
(6.58,12.24)
, 4.
(7.37,8.37)
, 5.
(6.37,5.37)
, 6.
(14.11,10.69)
.
The jumps are associated with bifurcation points which correspond to
there being two coincident roots of the polynomial
f(g)
, or that its discriminant vanishes. The boundaries
of the regions where there are jumps from 2 to 0 or 2 to 4 correspond to
\tilde{n}_{f}
linked by (
161
) though
the fixed point couplings have opposite signs.
At the fixed points in general the couplings do not give potentials which are bounded below except for
one which matches (
164
) when
\tilde{n}_{f}
is large. The positivity condition remains satisfied
as
\tilde{n}_{f}
is reduced until just above the upper bifurcation point.

Diagrams showing the structure of fixed point solutions outlined above are presented in appendix
C
.

For the stability matrix eigenvalues the absence of
g,\lambda
contributions to the
Yukawa
\beta
-function at lowest order ensures that one eigenvalue is
\varepsilon
for any
n,\tilde{n}_{f}

and the remaining eigenvalues are obtained from

M=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\lambda}{\hat{\beta}}_{\lambda}&\partial_{g}\beta_{\lambda}\\
\partial_{\lambda}\beta_{g}&\partial_{g}{\hat{\beta}}_{g}\end{pmatrix}\bigg|_{\lambda=\lambda_{*},g=g_{*},y=y_{*}}\,.

(166)

For
\tilde{n}_{f}
small from (
162
)

\kappa_{\pm}\sim-\varepsilon-\Big(9B+n_{s}\alpha-\beta\pm\sqrt{(9B-2\,n_{s}\alpha-\beta)^{2}+648A}\,\Big)\frac{2\,\tilde{n}_{f}}{(n_{s}\,\alpha+2\,\beta)^{2}}\,\,\varepsilon\,.

(167)

Starting from (
163
) the eigenvalues are

\kappa_{1}\sim-\frac{n_{s}-4}{n_{s}+8}\,\varepsilon+{\rm O}(\tilde{n}_{f})\,,\qquad\kappa_{2}\sim\varepsilon+\frac{6\big(n_{s}(n_{s}+10)\alpha+4\beta\big)}{(n_{s}+2)(n_{s}\alpha+2\,\beta)^{2}}\,\tilde{n}_{f}\,\varepsilon\,.

(168)

Otherwise for large
\tilde{n}_{f}
from the fixed point (
164
)

\kappa_{\pm}\sim\varepsilon+\Big(9B+n_{s}\alpha-\beta\pm\sqrt{(9B-2\,n_{s}\alpha-\beta)^{2}+648A}\,\Big)\frac{2}{\tilde{n}_{f}}\,\varepsilon\,.

(169)

For the supersymmetric case using (
132
) there are possible fixed points
in the
\varepsilon
expansion if
4\beta_{d}+3\alpha_{d}>0
. From the lower bound on

\beta_{d}
this is satisfied whenever
n_{s}>2
.

8.3
U(1)
Symmetry

A similar reduction is possible for complex scalar fields where there is at least an overall

U(1)
symmetry. In this case we consider chiral fermions
\psi

and
\chi
of opposite chirality which need not be equal in number.
Writing

y^{i}=y\,{\mathds{1}}_{m}t^{i}\,,\qquad{\bar{y}}_{i}={\bar{y}}\,{\mathds{1}}_{m}{\bar{t}}_{i}\,,\qquad{\bar{t}}_{i}=(t^{i})^{\dagger}\,,

(170)

then
t^{i}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, need not be a square matrix but is assumed to be
r\times s
.
In this case we assume

U_{-}{\!\!}^{-1}t^{i}\,U_{+}=R^{i}{}_{j}\,t^{j}\,,\quad U_{-}\in U(r)\,,\ U_{+}\in U(s)\,,\quad[R^{i}{}_{j}]\in H\subset U(n)\,.

(171)

Corresponding to the previous discussion we assume, with a choice of normalisation for
t^{i}
,

t^{i}\,{\bar{t}}_{i}=s\,{\mathds{1}}_{r}\,,\qquad{\bar{t}}_{i}\,t^{i}=r\,{\mathds{1}}_{s}\,,\qquad\mathrm{tr}(t_{i}\,{\bar{t}}^{j})=rs/n\,\delta_{i}{}^{j}\,.

(172)

Defining

\mathrm{tr}\big({\bar{t}}_{(i}\,t^{k}\,{\bar{t}}_{j)}\,t^{l}\big)=h_{ij}{}^{kl}\,,\qquad h_{ik}{}^{jk}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,rs(r+s)/n\,\delta_{i}{}^{j}\,,

(173)

the scalar coupling is assumed to have the form
6
6

6

The necessary conditions for a positive
potential can be obtained from

\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{t}}\,t)\big)^{2}\big/\min(r,s)\leq\mathrm{tr}({\bar{t}}\,t\,{\bar{t}}\,t)\leq\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{t}}\,t)\big)^{2}\,,

where
t
is a
r\times s
complex matrix and
{\bar{t}}
its hermitian conjugate.

\lambda_{ij}{}^{kl}=\lambda\big(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}+\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k}\big)+g\,h_{ij}{}^{kl}\,.

(174)

As above we assume conditions are imposed such that
\beta
-functions determining the RG
flow are reduced to
\beta_{y},\,\beta_{\lambda},\ \beta_{g}
with
\beta_{\bar{y}}=(\beta_{y})^{*}
and the anomalous dimension matrices
become
\gamma_{\varphi}\,{\mathds{1}}_{n},\gamma_{\psi}{\mathds{1}}_{r},\,\gamma_{\chi}{\mathds{1}}_{s}
with
\gamma_{\varphi}=\gamma_{\bar{\varphi}}
. Quadratic operators
are decomposed as

\varphi_{i}{\bar{\varphi}}^{j}=\sigma\,\delta_{i}{}^{j}+\rho_{i}{}^{j}\,,\ \ \rho_{i}{}^{i}=0\,,\quad\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}=(\varphi^{2})_{ij}\,,

(175)

and the corresponding anomalous dimension matrices are then of the general form

\displaystyle\gamma_{\sigma}\,\delta_{i}{}^{j}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\rho\hskip 1.5pti}{}^{j}{}_{k}{}^{l}=\gamma_{\rho}\,\big(\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{k}{}^{j}-\tfrac{1}{n}\delta_{i}{}^{j}\delta_{k}{}^{l}\big)+\gamma^{\prime}{\!\!}_{\rho}\,d_{ik}{}^{jl}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}{\!\!}_{\rho}\,w_{ik}{}^{jl}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\varphi^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt}{}_{ij}{}^{kl}=\gamma_{\varphi^{2}}\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}+\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k}\big)+\gamma^{\prime}{\!\!}_{\varphi^{2}}\,d_{ij}{}^{kl}\,,

(176)

where

\displaystyle h_{ij}{}^{kl}={}

\displaystyle q\big(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}+\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k}\big)+d_{ij}{}^{kl}\,,\qquad(n+1)q=\tfrac{1}{2}\,rs(r+s)/n\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\big({\bar{t}}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle[i$}\,t^{k}\,{\bar{t}}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle j]$}\,t^{l}\big)={}

\displaystyle p\big(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}-\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k}\big)+w_{ij}{}^{kl}\,,\qquad(n-1)p=\tfrac{1}{2}\,rs(r-s)/n\,,

(177)

so that both
d_{ij}{}^{kl}
and
w_{ij}{}^{kl}
are zero on contraction of up and down indices.
Instead of (
174
)

\lambda_{ij}{}^{kl}={\hat{\lambda}}\big(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}+\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k}\big)+g\,d_{ij}{}^{kl}\,,\qquad\hat{\lambda}=\lambda+q\,g\,.

(178)

The eigenvalues and corresponding degeneracies for the
d
and
w
tensors as they appear in (
176
)
are given in appendix
B
.

At one loop it is sufficient to impose the conditions

\displaystyle h_{ij}{}^{mn}h_{mn}{}^{kl}={}

\displaystyle\tilde{A}\big(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}+\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k}\big)+\tilde{B}\,h_{ij}{}^{kl}\,,

\displaystyle h_{m(i}{}^{n(k}h_{j)n}{}^{l)m}={}

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{\prime}\big(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}+\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k}\big)+\tilde{B}^{\prime}\,h_{ij}{}^{kl}\,.

(179)

Hence

\displaystyle\beta_{y}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\big(r+s+m\,{rs}/{n}\big)y^{2}{\bar{y}}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle 2(n+4)\lambda^{2}+4(n+1)q\,\lambda\,g+(\tilde{A}+4\tilde{A}^{\prime})g^{2}+2\,m\,rs/n\,\lambda\,y{\bar{y}}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{g}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle 12\,\lambda\,g+(\tilde{B}+4\tilde{B}^{\prime})g^{2}+2\,m\,rs/n\,g\,y{\bar{y}}-4\,m\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}\,.

(180)

A necessary condition is

h_{ik}{}^{mn}h_{mn}{}^{kj}=(n+1)\big(\tilde{A}+q\,\tilde{B}\big)\delta_{i}{}^{j}=\big(2(n+1)(\tilde{A}^{\prime}+q\,\tilde{B}^{\prime})-(n+1)^{2}q^{2}\big)\delta_{i}{}^{j}\,.

(181)

Defining

{\tilde{a}}=\tilde{A}+q\,\tilde{B}-2q^{2}\,,\qquad d_{ik}{}^{mn}d_{mn}{}^{kj}=(n+1){\tilde{a}}\,\delta_{i}{}^{j}\,,

(182)

then the one loop scalar
\beta
-function can be alternatively expressed as

\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hat{\lambda}}$}{}^{(1)}=2(n+4)\,{\hat{\lambda}}{}^{2}+3\,{\tilde{a}}\,g^{2}+m\,rs/n\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,y{\bar{y}}-4\,m\,q\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}\,.

(183)

For
{\tilde{a}}=0

d_{ij}{}^{kl}=0
and
h_{ij}{}^{kl}=q(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}+\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k})

so is no longer independent and the
g
coupling is redundant.

This framework encompasses a variety of theories discussed in the literature. As an illustration
we may consider
\{t^{a}\}
to be a basis for
r\times s
complex matrices where
n=rs
and which
satisfy (
172
). The scalar field symmetry group is
H=U(r)\times U(s)/U(1)
.
Positivity of the potential holds if

\lambda>0\,,\quad 2\,\lambda+g>0\quad\mbox{or}\quad g>0\,,\quad 2\,\lambda+g/\min(r,s)>0\,.

(184)

This example requires (
179
)

\tilde{A}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,,\qquad\tilde{B}=0\,,\qquad\tilde{A}^{\prime}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,,\qquad\tilde{B}^{\prime}=\tfrac{1}{4}(r+s)\,.

(185)

In this case from (
182
) and (
177
)

{\tilde{a}}=\tfrac{(r^{2}-1)(s^{2}-1)}{2(n+1)^{2}}\,,\qquad q=\tfrac{r+s}{2(n+1)}\,.

(186)

For the purely scalar theory, without fermions, results for
\beta
-functions have been obtained to
five loops in
Calabrese
and more recently to six loops in

Kompaniets
;
Bednyakov
and a bootstrap analysis has been undertaken in
StergiouU
.

The lowest order
\beta
-functions and anomalous dimensions are then, with
n=rs
,

\displaystyle\beta_{y}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(r+s+m)y{\bar{y}}\big)\,y\,,\quad\beta_{\lambda}{\!}^{(1)}=2(n+4)\lambda^{2}+2(r+s)\lambda\,g+\tfrac{3}{2}\,g^{2}+2m\,\lambda\,y{\bar{y}}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{g}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle 12\,g\,\lambda+(r+s)\,g^{2}+2m\,g\,y{\bar{y}}-4m\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\varphi}{\!}^{(1)}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}m\,y{\bar{y}}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\psi}{\!}^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{2}r\,y{\bar{y}}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\chi}{\!}^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{2}s\,y{\bar{y}}\,,

(187)

and in (
176
)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\sigma}{\!}^{(1)}=2(n+1)\,{\hat{\lambda}}+m\,y{\bar{y}}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\rho}{\!}^{(1)}=\gamma_{\varphi^{2}}{}^{(1)}=2\,{\hat{\lambda}}+m\,y{\bar{y}}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}{\!\!}_{\varphi^{2}}{}^{(1)}=\gamma^{\prime}{\!\!}_{\rho}{}^{(1)}=2\,g\,,\qquad\gamma^{\prime\prime}{\!\!}_{\rho}{}^{(1)}=0\,.

(188)

Setting
s=1
,
r=n
, so that
\tilde{a}=0
and the
d
-tensor vanishes, the
\beta
-function reduces to

\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hat{\lambda}}$}{}^{(1)}=2(n+4)\,{\hat{\lambda}}{}^{2}+2m\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,y{\bar{y}}-2m\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}\,.

(189)

For
n=1
this coincides with the the Nambu Jona-Lasinio extended
XY
model,
with
U(1)
symmetry when
m=1
, so long as
y{\bar{y}}\to 2\,y^{2}
.

At higher orders further relations corresponding to primitive diagrams are necessary. The
primitive Yukawa diagrams
2a,\ 2f
and scalar diagram
2g
correspond to

\displaystyle t^{j}{\bar{t}}_{k}\hskip 0.5ptt^{l}\,d_{jl}{}^{ki}=(n+1){\tilde{a}}\,t^{i}\,,\quad t^{j}{\bar{t}}_{k}\hskip 0.5ptt^{i}{\bar{t}}_{j}\hskip 0.5ptt^{k}=t^{i}\,,\quad\mathrm{tr}\big({\bar{t}}_{(i}\hskip 0.7ptt^{m}{\bar{t}}_{j)}\hskip 0.7ptt^{(k}{\bar{t}}_{m}\hskip 0.7ptt^{l)})=\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}+\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k}\big)\,.

(190)

The two loop 1PI contributions to the Yukawa and scalar
\beta
-functions are, with

{\tilde{a}},\,q
as in (
186
), then

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{y}{\!}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle\big({-2(r+s)}\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,y{\bar{y}}-2(n+1)\,{\tilde{a}}\,g\,y{\bar{y}}+2\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}\big)\,y\,,

\displaystyle\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hat{\lambda}}$}{}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle-4(5n+11){\hat{\lambda}}^{3}-6(n+7)\,{\tilde{a}}\,g^{2}{\hat{\lambda}}-4(n-5)q\,{\tilde{a}}\,g^{3}-2(n+4)m\,{\hat{\lambda}}^{2}\,y{\bar{y}}-3\,{\tilde{a}}\,m\,g^{2}\,y{\bar{y}}

\displaystyle{}+8\,q\,m\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}+4\,{\tilde{a}}\,m\,g\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}+4\big((r+s)q+1\big)m\,(y{\bar{y}})^{3}\,,

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}_{g}{\!}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle-12(n+7)\,g\,{\hat{\lambda}}^{2}-2(n-5)q\,g^{2}\big(12\,{\hat{\lambda}}+m\,y{\bar{y}}\big)-2\big(n+4-18(n-1)q^{2}\big)g^{3}

\displaystyle{}-12\,m\,g\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,y{\bar{y}}+8\,m\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}-8\,q\,m\,g\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}+4(r+s)m\,(y{\bar{y}})^{3}\,.

(191)

At two loops the anomalous dimensions are given by

\displaystyle\gamma_{\varphi}{\!}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}(n+1)\big({\hat{\lambda}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\tilde{a}}\,g^{2}\big)-\tfrac{3}{8}\,m\,(r+s)\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\psi}{\!}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{8}\,r(s+3m)\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\chi}{\!}^{(2)}=-\tfrac{1}{8}\,s(r+3m)\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\sigma}{\!}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle-6(n+1)\big({\hat{\lambda}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\tilde{a}}\,g^{2}\big)-2(n+1)\,m\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,y{\bar{y}}+2\,\gamma_{\varphi}{\!}^{(2)}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\varphi^{2}}{\!}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle-2(n+3)\,{\hat{\lambda}}^{2}-2\,{\tilde{a}}\,g^{2}-2\,m\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,y{\bar{y}}+4\,q\,m\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}+2\,\gamma_{\varphi}{\!}^{(2)}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}{\!\!}_{\varphi^{2}}{\!}{}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle-8\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,g-2(n-3)q\,g^{2}-2\,m\,g\,y{\bar{y}}+4\,m\,(y{\bar{y}})^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\rho}{\!}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle-2(n+3)\,{\hat{\lambda}}^{2}-\tfrac{n-3}{n-1}\,{\tilde{a}}\,g^{2}-2\,m\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,y{\bar{y}}+2\,\gamma_{\varphi}{\!}^{(2)}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}{\!\!}_{\rho}{}^{(2)}={}

\displaystyle-8\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,g-(n-7)q\,g^{2}-2\,m\,g\,y{\bar{y}}\,,\qquad\gamma^{\prime\prime}{\!\!}_{\rho}{}^{(2)}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,(r-s)\,g^{2}\,.

(192)

At three loops further relations are necessary. Corresponding to
3{\tilde{z}}

t^{j}{\bar{t}}_{k}\hskip 0.5ptt^{l}{\bar{t}}_{j}\hskip 0.5ptt^{i}{\bar{t}}_{l}\hskip 0.5ptt^{k}+t^{k}{\bar{t}}_{l}\hskip 0.5ptt^{i}{\bar{t}}_{j}\hskip 0.5ptt^{l}{\bar{t}}_{k}\hskip 0.5ptt^{j}=(r+s)\,t^{i}\,.

(193)

For just the quartic scalar coupling at three loops then corresponding to
3f
there is the relation

\displaystyle d_{m(i}{}^{nr}\,d_{j)r}{}^{pq}\,d_{ps}{}^{m(k}\,d_{nq}{}^{l)s}={\cal A}(\delta_{i}{}^{k}\delta_{j}{}^{l}+\delta_{i}{}^{l}\delta_{j}{}^{k})+{\tilde{c}}\,d_{ij}{}^{kl}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}=\tfrac{1}{8}\big(n+4-18\,q^{2}\big)\,{\tilde{a}}\,,\qquad{\tilde{c}}=-\tfrac{1}{8}\,(5\,n+29)\,q+2(5\,n-1)\,q^{3}\,.

(194)

For the purely scalar theory obtained by setting the Yukawa couplings to zero there are non trivial fixed points
which to lowest order have the form, for
r,s>1
,

g_{*\pm}=\frac{n-2}{D_{rs}\pm 3\sqrt{R_{rs}}}\,\varepsilon\,,\qquad{\hat{\lambda}}_{*\pm}=\frac{D_{rs}\pm(n+1)\sqrt{R_{rs}}}{4(n+1)(D_{rs}\pm 3\sqrt{R_{rs}})}\,\varepsilon\,,

(195)

for

D_{rs}=(n-5)(r+s)\,,\qquad R_{rs}=r^{2}+s^{2}-10\,rs+24\,.

(196)

Since
R_{rs}=(r-n_{+}(s))(r-n_{-}(s))
for
n_{\pm}(s)=5s\pm 2\sqrt{6}\sqrt{s^{2}-1}

then
R_{rs}\geq 0
and there are real fixed points if
r\geq n_{+}(s)
or
r\leq n_{-}(s)
. For
r>s

only the first case is relevant.
The corresponding stability matrix eigenvalues at the fixed points in (
195
) are then

\big(\kappa_{1\pm},\,\kappa_{2\pm}\big)=\Big(1,\pm\tfrac{(n-2)\sqrt{R_{rs}}}{D_{rs}\pm 3\sqrt{R_{rs}}}\Big)\varepsilon\,.

(197)

Integer solutions for the bifurcation points when
R_{rs}=0
can be obtained iteratively, for
r>s
,
by taking
r_{i}=10\,r_{i-1}-s_{i-1},\ s_{i}=r_{i-1}
starting from
r_{1}=5,\ s_{1}=1
.
This scalar theory with
H=U(r)\times U(s)/U(1)
symmetry is an obvious extension of the bifundamental
theory with real scalars and
O(m)\times O(n)/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry discussed recently in

Seeking
;
RychkovS
which contains earlier citations. Defining the invariants

||\lambda||^{2}=\lambda_{ij}{}^{kl}\lambda_{kl}{}^{ij}=2n(n+1)\big({\hat{\lambda}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\tilde{a}}\,g^{2}\big)\,,\qquad|\lambda_{*\pm}|=\lambda_{ij}{}^{ij}=n(n+1)\,{\hat{\lambda}}\,,

(198)

then at the fixed point (
195
)

||\lambda_{*\pm}||^{2}=\tfrac{1}{24}\,n\big(\varepsilon^{2}-\kappa_{2\pm}{\!}^{2}\big)\,,\qquad|\lambda_{*\pm}|=\tfrac{1}{4}\,n\big(\varepsilon+\kappa_{2\pm}\big)\,.

(199)

Clearly
||\lambda_{*\pm}||^{2}\leq\tfrac{1}{24}\,n\,\varepsilon^{2}

in accord, of course, with the bound obtained by Hogervorst and Toldo
Hogervorst
extending
the results in
RychkovS
. For any
n
there is also the Heisenberg fixed point with
O(2n)
symmetry
where

{\hat{\lambda}}_{*H}=\frac{1}{2(n+4)}\,\varepsilon\,\qquad g_{*H}=0\,,

(200)

and

\big(\kappa_{1H},\kappa_{2H}\big)=\Big(1,\tfrac{2-n}{n+4}\Big)\varepsilon\,,\quad||\lambda_{*H}||^{2}=\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2(n+4)^{2}}\,\varepsilon^{2}\,,\quad|\lambda_{*H}|=\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2(n+4)}\,\varepsilon\,.

(201)

For
n=2
these results coincide with (
195
), (
197
) and (
199
).

For non zero Yukawa couplings the one loop
\beta
function requires

(y{\bar{y}})_{*}=\varepsilon/(r+s+m)\,.

(202)

After using (
202
) the equations determining
\lambda_{*},\,g_{*}
are then invariant under

m\to\frac{(r+s)^{2}}{m}\,,\qquad\varepsilon\to-\varepsilon\,,

(203)

relating results for large and small
m
. At
m=m_{c}
for
m_{c}=r+s
there are no solutions
if
2(n+4)\lambda^{2}+2(r+s)\lambda g+\frac{3}{2}g^{2}>0
or

2n\leq r^{2}+s^{2}<n+12\,.

(204)

This is rather restrictive. Taking
r,s\geq 2
and
r\leq s

the only possibilities for
(r,s)
are just
(2,2),\,(2,3),\,(3,3),\,(2,4)
(in the last case
r^{2}+s^{2}=n+12
).
In these cases where
R_{rs}<0
there are two fixed
points for small and large
m
but none over some interval centred on
r+s
, the interval shrinks to
zero in the
(2,4)
case where the bound in (
204
) is saturated.
Otherwise for
R_{rs}<0
there are again two fixed points for small and large
m
but four over
a region centred on
r+s
. If
R_{rs}\geq 0
there are four fixed points for any
m
.

At large
m
there is a fixed point which is the counterpart of the Gaussian fixed point for small
m

and gives rise to a positive semi-definite potential and positive stability matrix eigenvalues

g_{*}\sim\frac{4}{m}\,\varepsilon-(r+s)\frac{16}{m^{2}}\,\varepsilon\,,\quad\lambda_{*}\sim-\frac{24}{m^{2}}\,\varepsilon\,,\quad\kappa_{\pm}\sim\varepsilon+\big(r+s\pm 4\big)\frac{6}{m}\,\varepsilon\,.

(205)

For
s=1
and
r=n
there is just one scalar coupling and at lowest order

{\hat{\lambda}}_{*\pm}=\frac{1}{4(n+4)}\Big(-\frac{m-n-1}{m+n+1}\pm\sqrt{\tilde{Z}}\Big)\varepsilon\,,\quad{\tilde{Z}}=\frac{(m-n-1)^{2}+16(n+4)m}{(m+n+1)^{2}}\,.

(206)

For
n=1
this is identical to the
XY
case as given by (
143
) and (
144
).

9
Consistency Relations

The existence of an
a
-function requires consistency relations between the coefficients for individual
non primitive graphs appearing in the
\beta
-functions and the anomalous dimensions. The basic equation
has the form, for couplings
\{g^{I}\}

Jack:2013sha
,

\partial_{I}A=T_{IJ}\,B^{J}\,,

(207)

where
A
is constructed from 1PI and 1VI vacuum diagrams,
T_{IJ}
also from 1PI, 1VI vacuum diagrams
with two vertices identified and

B^{I}=\beta^{I}-(\upsilon g)^{I}\,,

(208)

with
\upsilon(g)
corresponding to an element of the Lie algebra of the maximal symmetry group of
the Lagrangian kinetic term. In general for a vanishing trace of the energy momentum tensor and hence
conformal symmetry the requirement is that
B^{I}=0
. In (
207
)
T_{IJ}
need not be symmetric,
although any antisymmetric part has further constraints.
In (
208
)
\upsilon(g)
is necessarily present starting at 3 loops.
There is a freedom in (
207
) where

A\sim A+g_{IJ}B^{I}B^{J}\,,\qquad T_{IJ}\sim T_{IJ}+{\mathcal{L}}_{B}\,g_{IJ}+\partial_{I}(g_{JK}B^{K})-\partial_{J}(g_{IK}B^{K})\,,

(209)

for arbitrary symmetric
g_{IJ}
. This does not preserve the symmetry of
T_{IJ}
.

In the present context the lowest order contribution to
T_{IJ}
is first present at two loops for the Yukawa
couplings,
T_{yy}
, and at three for the scalar quartic couplings,
T_{\lambda\lambda}
.
In consequence (
207
) provides potential relations between the Yukawa
\beta
-functions and fermion, scalar anomalous
dimensions at
\ell
loops and the scalar coupling
\beta
-function at
\ell-1
loops where
A
involves
\ell+2
loop
diagrams. Eliminating
A
and
T_{IJ}
ensures that the relations contain non linear contributions involving
the
\beta
-functions and anomalous dimensions at lower loop order. The elimination of any particular
contribution to
A
is possible when the relevant diagram is not vertex transitive. If the diagram has
n
inequivalent
vertices then (
207
) leads to
n
independent equations in this case.
For
\ell=2,3
, and including also arbitrary gauge couplings, the possible relations
were exhaustively analysed by Poole and Thomsen
Poole:2019kcm
.
At this order the conditions relate contributions to the

\beta
-functions and anomalous dimensions which have insertions of one loop triangles and one loop bubbles.

For
\ell=2
there are 11 5-loop vacuum diagrams for
A
(3 are vertex transitive)
and 9 possible 3 loop
T_{IJ}
, all of which are symmetric,
and (
207
) gives rise to 21 equations.
Nevertheless there are 4 conditions on the individual
\beta
-function coefficients which reduce to the
vanishing of

\displaystyle B_{1}=\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}-3\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1b}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}\,,\qquad B_{2}=2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}+2\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}\,,

\displaystyle B_{3}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}{\!}^{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\big(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\big)-\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}{\!}^{2}\,\big(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}\big)\,,

\displaystyle B_{4}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}{\!}^{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}(\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2c})+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\big(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}\big)\,,

(210)

or inserting one loop results this gives the conditions

\displaystyle\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}=-24\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}\,,\quad\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}=2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}-\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\,,\quad 4\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}=16\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}-4\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\,,

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}=4\,(\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b})-2\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\,.

(211)

The non planar
\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}
is not present since the associated vacuum graph obtained by joining the
external lines is vertex transitive.

For
\ell=3
there are, for a general renormalisable fermion scalar theory 49 5 loop diagrams for
A
(6 are vertex
transitive) and for
T_{yy}
there are 33 distinct contributions for
T_{yy}
which are symmetric and 20 with no symmetry. (
207
) then generates 152 equations which reduce to 42 conditions on the
\beta
-function,
anomalous dimension coefficients. We consider first relations for non planar contributions
to the
\beta
-function and anomalous dimension where there are 7 relations due to the vanishing of

\displaystyle C_{1}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{w}}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{x}}\,,\qquad C_{2}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\big(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{o}}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{p}}\big)-\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\big(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{s}}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{u}}\big)\,,

\displaystyle C_{3}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\big(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{g}}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{v}}\big)+\big(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2d}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2e}\big)\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}\,,

\displaystyle C_{4}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3p}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\big(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{s}}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{v}}\big)-\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}\,,

\displaystyle C_{5}=\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{o}}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\big(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{g}}-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{r}}\big)-\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2c}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}\,,

\displaystyle S_{5}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}{}^{2}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3A}-\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}(2\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3D}-\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3F}+\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3G})+2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}{}^{2}(\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3I}-\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3J})

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt{}+\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2A}(2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2B}-\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2A})\,,

\displaystyle S_{6}=2\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}{}^{2}\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3B}-\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}(2\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3A}-\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3C}+2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3D})+2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}{}^{2}(2\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3D}-\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3K}+\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3G})

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt{}-2\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}(\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2A}{}^{2}+\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2B}\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt2A})\,,

\displaystyle S_{7}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}{}^{2}\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3C}-2\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}(\beta_{3C}+\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3F})+4\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}{}^{2}\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3K}-4\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2A}\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt2B}=0\,,

(219)

and for the non planar diagrams just 2

\displaystyle S_{8}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3L}-\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}(\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3N}-2\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3O})-2\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2C}\,\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2A}\,,

\displaystyle S_{9}=\beta_{Y\hskip 0.2pt1}\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt3I}+\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt1}(\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3M}-\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt3O})-\beta_{Y\hskip 0.5pt2C}\,\gamma_{\Phi\hskip 0.5pt2B}\,.

(220)

10
Scheme Variations for Scalar Fermion Theory to Three Loops

The coefficients appearing in the expansions of the
\beta
-functions and anomalous dimensions
for a general scalar fermion theory are in general dependent on the choice of regularisation scheme.
At
\ell
loops possible scheme variations in
\gamma_{\phi}{\!}^{(\ell)},\,\gamma_{\psi}{\!}^{(\ell)},\,{\tilde{\beta}}_{y}{\!}^{(\ell)},\,{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda}{\!}^{(\ell)}
are determined in terms of arbitrary
parameters related to the expansions of
\gamma_{\phi}{\!}^{(\ell-1)},\,\gamma_{\psi}{\!}^{(\ell-1)},\,{\tilde{\beta}}_{y}{\!}^{(\ell-1)},\,{\tilde{\beta}}_{\lambda}{\!}^{(\ell-1)}
. This depends on preserving the form of
the functions
{\tilde{\beta}},\gamma
in terms of contributions corresponding to 1PI and 1VI diagrams.
Labelling the coefficients
\alpha
in the expansion at
\ell
loops by
{\rm g},\ell,r
, where here

{\rm g}=\phi,\psi,y,\lambda
,
the general forms of the variations for
\alpha_{g}\equiv\alpha_{{\rm g}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}
, with
\alpha\to\gamma
for

{\rm g}=\phi,\,\psi
and
\alpha\to\beta
for
{\rm g}\to y,\,\lambda
, are shown in appendix
E

to involve a sum over contributions

X_{g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}}=-X_{g^{\prime\prime},g^{\prime}}=\alpha_{g^{\prime}}\,\epsilon_{g^{\prime\prime}}-\epsilon_{g^{\prime}}\,\alpha_{g^{\prime\prime}}\,,\qquad g^{\prime}={\rm g}^{\prime}\hskip 0.8pt\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}\,,\quad g^{\prime\prime}={\rm g}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 0.8pt\ell^{\prime\prime}r^{\prime\prime}\,,

(221)

where
\{\epsilon_{g}\}
are arbitrary parameters, so that

\delta\alpha_{g}=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{3}{g',g''}{\ell'+\ell''=\ell, \gv'' = \gv}}\hskip-8.5359pt{\mathcal{N}}_{g}{}^{g^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime}}\,X_{g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}}\,,

(222)

and
{\mathcal{N}}_{g}{}^{g^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime}}=-{\mathcal{N}}_{g}{}^{g^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime}}
are integer coefficients. Of course one
loop coefficients are scheme invariant and higher loops coefficients corresponding to primitive diagrams,
which have a different topology and do not lead to integrals which have subdivergences, are also scheme
invariant. Any
e^{g}
such that for a given
\ell

\sum_{g,\,\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell}\hskip-5.69046pte^{g}\,{\mathcal{N}}_{g}{}^{g^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime}}=0\,,

(223)

gives rise to a linear
\ell
loop scheme invariant
\sum_{g}e^{g}\alpha_{g}
.

At two loops there are 10 possible
X
’s but only 7 appear in scheme variations as

X_{\phi 1,\lambda 1b}
,
X_{\psi 1,\lambda 1a}
,
X_{y1,\lambda 1a}
, are not present, so we have

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt2a}=\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt2b}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt2a}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt2d}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt2e}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt2f}=\delta\beta_{\lambda\hskip 0.5pt2a}=\delta\beta_{\lambda\hskip 0.5pt2g}=0\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt2b}=4\,X_{\psi 1,\phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt2c}=2\,X_{y1,\phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt2a}=2\,X_{\phi 1,\psi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt2c}=2\,X_{y1,\psi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt2b}=2\,X_{\phi 1,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt2c}=2\,X_{\psi 1,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{\lambda\hskip 0.5pt2b}=4\,X_{\phi 1,\lambda 1a}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{\lambda\hskip 0.5pt2c}=2\,X_{\lambda 1b,\lambda 1a}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{\lambda\hskip 0.5pt2d}=X_{\lambda 1b,\lambda 1a}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{\lambda\hskip 0.5pt2e}=2\,X_{\psi 1,\lambda 1b}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{\lambda\hskip 0.5pt2f}=X_{y1,\lambda 1b}\,.

(224)

The cases
y2a,\,y2f,\,\lambda 2g
correspond to primitive diagrams and so the variation is
necessarily zero. Apart from those coefficients which are individually invariant there are four
linear scheme invariants.

At three loops the results for scheme variations separate into different groups.
There are six primitive three loop diagrams for the Yukawa
\beta
-function so that

\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3f}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3l}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{w}}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{x}}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{y}}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{z}}=0\,,

(225)

and there are cases where the individual terms are scheme invariant

\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3j}=\delta\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{f}$}=\delta\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{l}$}=\delta\beta_{y,3\tilde{n}}=0\,.

(226)

For those diagrams containing
the non planar subgraph corresponding to
\beta_{y2f}
the variations are

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3s}=2\,X_{\phi 1,y\hskip 0.5pt2f}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{o}}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{p}}=2\,X_{\psi 1,y\hskip 0.5pt2f}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3m}=2\,X_{y2f,\phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3p}=2\,X_{y2f,\psi 1}\,,

(227a)

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{g}}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{v}}=X_{y\hskip 0.5pt2f,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{r}}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{s}}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{u}}=X_{y1,y\hskip 0.5pt2f}\,.

(227b)

There are evidently 3 linear invariants from (
227a
) and 4 more from (
227b
).
Otherwise for the variations of the anomalous dimension contributions arising from planar diagrams we have

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3a}=6\,X_{\lambda 1a,\phi 2a}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3b}=6\,X_{\phi 1,\phi 2a}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3c}=3\,X_{\lambda 1b,\phi 2a}+X_{y2a,\phi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3d}=2\,X_{\phi 1,\phi 2b}+4\,X_{\psi 2a,\phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3f}=4\,X_{\psi 1,\phi 2b}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3g}=2\,X_{\psi 1,\phi 2b}+4\,X_{\psi 2b,\phi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3h}=2\,X_{\psi 1,\phi 2b}\,,

(228a)

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3e}=2\,X_{\phi 1,\phi 2c}+2\,X_{y2b,\phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3i}=4\,X_{\psi 2c,\phi 1}+2\,X_{y1,\phi 2b}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3j}=4\,X_{\psi 1,\phi 2c}+X_{y1,\phi 2b}+2\,X_{y2c,\phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3k}=2\,X_{y1,\phi 2c}+2\,X_{y2e,\phi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\phi\hskip 0.5pt3l}=2\,X_{y1,\phi 2c}+4\,X_{y2d,\phi 1}\,,

(228b)

and

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3a}=2\,X_{\phi 2a,\psi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3b}=2\,X_{y2a,\psi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3c}=4\,X_{\phi 1,\psi 2a}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3d}=2\,X_{\psi 1,\psi 2a}+2\,X_{\phi 1,\psi 2b}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3e}=2\,X_{\phi 1,\psi 2b}+2\,X_{\psi 2a,\psi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3g}=4\,X_{\psi 1,\psi 2a}+2\,X_{\phi 2b,\psi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3i}=4\,X_{\psi 1,\psi 2b}\,,

(229a)

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3f}=2\,X_{\phi 1,\psi 2c}+X_{y1,\psi 2a}+X_{y2b,\psi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3h}=2\,X_{y1,\psi 2a}+2\,X_{\phi 2c,\psi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3k}=2\,X_{y1,\psi 2c}+2\,X_{y2d,\psi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3l}=2\,X_{\psi 2c,\psi 1}+2\,X_{y1,\psi 2b}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3m}=X_{y1,\psi 2c}+X_{y2e,\psi 1}+X_{y2d,\psi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3n}=2\,X_{\psi 1,\psi 2c}+X_{y1,\psi 2b}+X_{y2c,\psi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3o}=2\,X_{\psi 1,\psi 2c}+2\,X_{y2c,\psi 1}\,.

(229b)

The remaining scheme variations are then

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3b}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3c}=X_{\lambda 1a,y2a}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3d}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3e}=2\,X_{\phi 1,y2a}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3j}=2\,X_{\psi 1,y2a}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3w}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3x}=X_{\lambda 1b,y2a}\,,

(230a)

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3a}=2\,X_{\phi 2a,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3g}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3h}=X_{y2a,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3i}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3k}=X_{y1,y2a}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3m}=4\,X_{\phi 1,y2b}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3n}=2\,X_{\phi 1,y2e}+X_{y2b,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3o}=2\,X_{\phi 1,y2e}+X_{y1,y2b}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3p}=2\,X_{\phi 1,y2c}+2\,X_{\psi 1,y2b}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3q}=2\,X_{\phi 1,y2d}+X_{y2b,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3r}=2\,X_{\phi 1,y2d}+X_{y1,y2b}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3t}=2\,X_{\phi 1,y2c}+2\,X_{\psi 2a,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3u}=4\,X_{\psi 1,y2b}+2\,X_{\phi 2b,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3v}=2\,X_{y1,y2b}+2\,X_{\phi 2c,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3y}=\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3z}=4\,X_{\psi 1,y2c}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{a}}=2\,X_{\psi 1,y2c}+2\,X_{\psi 2b,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{b}$}=2\,X_{\psi 1,y2e}+X_{y1,y2c}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{c}}=2\,X_{\psi 1,y2e}+X_{y2c,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{d}$}=X_{y1,y2e}+X_{y2d,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{e}}=2\,X_{\psi 2c,y1}+2\,X_{y1,y2c}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{h}$}=\delta\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{i}$}=2\,X_{\psi 1,y2d}+X_{y1,y2c}\,,\quad\delta\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{j}$}=\delta\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{k}$}=2\,X_{\psi 1,y2d}+X_{y2c,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{m}}=X_{y1,y2d}+X_{y2e,y1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt3\tilde{q}}=X_{y1,y2e}+X_{y1,y2d}\,,\quad\delta\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{t}$}=2\,X_{y1,y2d}\,.

(230b)

Each variation is necessarily such that the number of fermion loops is conserved.
The variations in (
227a
), (
228a
), (
229a
) and (
230a
), apart from
that for
\beta_{y3x}
, correspond to the restriction to the
U(1)
invariant case.
The 21 variations in (
228a
), (
229a
) and (
230a
) involve 14 different
X
’s
and there are 7 linear invariants. The 40 variations in (
228b
), (
229b
) and (
230b
) involve 23 different
X
’s but there are 18 linear invariants since the equations
are invariant under

\delta X_{\phi 1,\psi 2c}=\delta X_{\psi 1,y2b}=\rho\,,\quad\delta X_{\phi 1,y2c}=\delta X_{\psi 1,\phi 2c}=\delta X_{y1,\psi 2a}=-\rho\,,\quad\delta X_{y1,\phi 2b}=2\rho\,.

(231)

Individual coefficients in the expansions of
\beta
or
\gamma
, besides those corresponding to primitive diagrams,
are scheme invariant when the associated vertex or propagator
subgraphs are a nested sequence all of the same form. Examples appear in (
226
).
In this case
\gamma_{\psi 1},\,\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt2b},\,\gamma_{\psi\hskip 0.5pt3j}
correspond to rainbow diagrams and
\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt1},\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt2e},\,\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{f}$}
correspond to vertex
ladder diagrams. For these cases there are exact all orders results

Delbourgo2
;
Broadhurst2
obtained by solving quadratic equations

\displaystyle\gamma_{\psi}\big|_{\rm rainbow}={}

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+y^{2}}-1=\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{2}-\tfrac{1}{8}\,y^{4}+\tfrac{1}{16}\,y^{6}-\tfrac{5}{128}\,y^{8}+\dots\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{y}/y\big|_{\rm ladder}={}

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+4\,y^{2}}-1=2\,y^{2}-2\,y^{4}+4\,y^{6}-10\,y^{8}+\dots\,.

(232)

Further sequences of nested diagrams are also associated with
\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt1},\,\beta_{y\hskip 0.5pt2d}

and
\beta\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle y{\hskip 0.5pt}3\tilde{l}$}
or
\beta_{y,3\tilde{n}}
so these are
necessarily scheme invariant.

For the restriction to the
U(1)
theory discussed in section 6, the scheme variations in
(
224
), (
228a
), (
229a
), (
230a
)
consistently restrict as they only involve the
\gamma
and
\beta
-function coefficients relevant in that case.
The sequence of nested diagrams for
\gamma_{\psi}\big|_{\rm rainbow}
remains in this case.
The scheme variations may be restricted to
{\mathcal{N}}=1
and
{\mathcal{N}}=\tfrac{1}{2}
supersymmetry. They are
consistent with the various constraints obtained earlier so long as all lower order conditions are
imposed. For the latter case at two loops

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\Phi 2B}=2\,X_{Y1,\Phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{Y2A}=2\,X_{\Phi 1,Y1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\Phi 2A}=\delta\beta_{Y2B}=\delta\beta_{Y2C}=0\,,

(233)

and at three loops individual scheme invariant coefficients correspond to

\delta\gamma_{\Phi 3C}=\delta\beta_{Y3H}=\delta\beta_{Y3J}=\delta\beta_{Y3P}=\delta\beta_{Y3Q}=0\,,

(234)

with
\beta_{Y3P},\ \beta_{Y3Q}
arising from primitive diagrams while
\gamma_{\Phi 3C}
, along with

\gamma_{\Phi 1},\,\gamma_{\Phi 2A}
, forms part of a sequence of nested rainbow diagrams.
For planar contributions

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\Phi 3A}=2\,\delta\gamma_{\Phi 3B}=4\,X_{\Phi 1,\Phi 2A}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\Phi 3D}=4\,X_{\Phi 1,\Phi 2B}+X_{Y1,\Phi 2A}+2\,X_{Y2A,\Phi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\Phi 3E}=2\,X_{\Phi 1,\Phi 2B}+2\,X_{Y2A,\Phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\Phi 3F}=4\,X_{\Phi 2B,\Phi 1}+2\,X_{Y1,\Phi 2A}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\Phi 3G}=2\,X_{Y1,\Phi 2B}+4\,X_{Y2B,\Phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\gamma_{\Phi 3H}=2\,X_{Y1,\Phi 2B}+2\,X_{Y2B,\Phi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{Y3A}=\delta\beta_{Y3B}=4\,X_{\Phi 1,Y2A}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{Y3C}=4\,X_{\Phi 1,Y2A}+2\,X_{\Phi 2A,Y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{Y3D}=\delta\beta_{Y3G}=2\,X_{\Phi 1,Y2B}+X_{Y1,Y2A}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{Y3E}=\delta\beta_{Y3F}=2\,X_{\Phi 1,Y2B}+X_{Y2A,Y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{Y3I}=2\,X_{Y1,Y2B}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{Y3K}=2\,X_{Y1,Y2A}+2\,X_{\Phi 2B,Y1}\,,\quad

(235)

and for non planar

\displaystyle\delta\gamma_{\Phi 3I}=2\,X_{Y2C,\Phi 1}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{Y3L}=4\,X_{\Phi 1,Y2C}\,,

\displaystyle\delta\beta_{Y3M}=X_{Y1,Y2C}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{Y3N}=2\,X_{Y1,Y2C}\,,\quad\delta\beta_{Y3O}=X_{Y2C,Y1}\,.

(236)

From (
233
) there is one linear invariant,
\gamma_{\Phi 2B}+\beta_{Y2A}
. (
235
)
contains 8 independent
X
’s and 15 equations giving 7 linear invariants
whereas in (
236
) 5 equations and 2
X
’s lead to 3 linear invariants.

These results may be used to verify the invariance of the consistency conditions obtained in section 8.
The variations are either identically zero, using the antisymmetry of
X
, or lead to antisymmetrised
products of three
X
’s,

Y_{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}}=\alpha_{g_{1}}\,X_{g_{2},g_{3}}+\alpha_{g_{3}}\,X_{g_{1},g_{2}}+\alpha_{g_{2}}\,X_{g_{3},g_{1}}\,,

(237)

with
g_{i}={\rm g}_{i}\ell_{i}r_{i}
. Given the definition of
X
in (
221
) necessarily
Y_{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}}=0

and, from the antisymmetry of
X_{g_{1},g_{2}}
, there is the identity

\alpha_{g_{1}}\,Y_{g_{2},g_{3},g_{4}}-\alpha_{g_{2}}\,Y_{g_{3},g_{4},g_{1}}+\alpha_{g_{3}}\,Y_{g_{4},g_{1},g_{2}}-\alpha_{g_{4}}\,Y_{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}}=0\,,

(238)

and hence

\alpha_{g_{1}}\,Y_{g_{2},g_{3},g_{4}}-\alpha_{g_{2}}\,Y_{g_{3},g_{4},g_{1}}\not\supset X_{g_{3},g_{4}}\,.

(239)

Apart from linear invariants there are also possible quadratic invariants

Q_{\kappa}=\kappa^{g_{1}g_{2}}\alpha_{g_{1}}\alpha_{g_{2}}\,,\quad\kappa^{g_{1}g_{2}}=\kappa^{g_{2}g_{1}}\,,

(240)

if
\kappa^{g_{1}g_{2}}
is such that

{\textstyle\sum_{g}}\;\kappa^{g_{1}g}{\mathcal{N}}_{g}{}^{g_{2}g_{3}}=F^{g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}}=F^{[g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}]}\,,

(241)

as then
\delta Q_{\kappa}=\frac{2}{3}\,\sum_{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}}\,F^{g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}}\,Y_{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}}
.
Higher order invariants are also possible as demonstrated later.

Applying this for (
224
) there are two quadratic invariants obtained from
Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y_{1}}
and

Y_{\psi 1,y1,\lambda 1b}
. However as a consequence of (
239
)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\psi 1}

\displaystyle Y_{\phi 1,\lambda 1a,\lambda 1b}-\beta_{\lambda 1a}Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,\lambda 1b}

\displaystyle{}=\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(\gamma_{\phi 1}\,X_{\lambda 1a,\lambda 1b}+\beta_{\lambda 1b}\,X_{\phi 1,\lambda 1a}\big)-\beta_{\lambda 1a}\big(\gamma_{\phi 1}\,X_{\psi 1,\lambda 1b}+\beta_{\lambda 1b}\,X_{\phi 1,\psi 1}\big)\,,

(242)

leads to a further cubic invariant. At the next order
from (
227a
), (
227b
) there are three possible
Y
’s,

Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2f}
,
Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2f}
,
Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2f}
, which would lead to three potential
quadratic invariants. Nevertheless these are not independent due to (
238
) and so there remain
two quadratic invariants for the non planar coefficients.
From (
228a
), (
229a
) and (
230a
) we can construct

\displaystyle Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,g}\,,\qquad g=\phi 2a,\,\phi 2b,\,\psi 2a,\,\psi 2b,\,y2a\,,

\displaystyle Y_{\phi 1,\lambda 1a,g},\ Y_{\psi 1,\lambda 1b,g},\ Y_{\lambda 1a,\lambda 1b,g}\,,\qquad g=\phi 2a,\,y2a\,,

(243)

which would appear to give 11 quadratic invariants.
These are not independent due to the identity

\displaystyle\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(\gamma_{\phi 1}Y_{\lambda 1a,\lambda 1b,g}+\beta_{\lambda 1b}\,Y_{\phi 1,\lambda 1a,g}\big)-\beta_{\lambda 1a}\,\big(\gamma_{\phi 1}Y_{\psi 1,\lambda 1b,g}+\beta_{\lambda 1b}Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,g}\big)

\displaystyle{}=\alpha_{g}\big(\gamma_{\psi 1}Y_{\phi 1,\lambda 1a,\lambda 1b}-\beta_{\lambda 1a}\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,\lambda 1b}\big)\,,\quad g=\phi 2a,y2a\,,\ \alpha_{g}=\gamma_{\phi 2a},\beta_{y2a}\,,

(244)

so there remain 9 quadratic invariants when
U(1)
symmetry is imposed.
In the general case there are
additional invariants flowing from (
228b
), (
229b
) and (
230b
).

\displaystyle Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,g}\,,\ Y_{\phi 1,y1,g}\,,\ Y_{\psi 1,y1,g}\,,\,\quad g=\phi 2c,\ \psi 2c,\ y2b,\ y2c,\ y2d,y2e\,,

(245a)

\displaystyle Y_{\phi 1,y1,g}\,,\ Y_{\psi 1,y1,g}\,,\qquad g=\phi 2a,\,\phi 2b,\,\psi 2a,\,\psi 2b,\ y2a\,.

(245b)

These are not independent due to (
238
). In (
245a
) we may then reduce to two sets of 6 and in (
245b
) to
one set of 5. Possibilities are further restricted by requiring results are independent of
\rho
in (
231
)
which leaves 16. There are then 11 independent
Y
’s for which there is no constraint, a possible basis is given by

\displaystyle Y_{\phi 1,y1,\phi 2a},\ Y_{\phi 1,y1,\phi 2c},\ Y_{\phi 1,y1,\psi 2b},\ Y_{\psi 1,y1,\psi 2c},\ Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2a},\ Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2b},

\displaystyle Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2c},\ Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2d},\ Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2d},\ Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2e},\ Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2e}\,,

(246)

and 5 involving pairs of
Y
’s formed from
Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,g}
or
Y_{\psi 1,y1,g}
,
g=\phi 2c,\,y2b
,

Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,g}
or
Y_{\phi 1,y1,g}
,
g=\psi 2c,\,y2c
,

Y_{\phi 1,y1,g}
or
Y_{\psi 1,y1,g}
,
g=\psi 2a,\,\phi 2b

which are
\rho
invariant. To achieve this (
231
) implies

\displaystyle-\delta Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,\phi 2c}=\delta Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2b}=-\delta Y_{\phi 1,y1,\psi 2a}=\gamma_{\phi 1}\,\rho\,,\qquad\delta Y_{\phi 1,y1,\phi 2b}=2\,\gamma_{\phi 1}\,\rho\,,

\displaystyle-\delta Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,\psi 2c}=\delta Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2c}=-\delta Y_{\psi 1,y1,\psi 2a}=\gamma_{\psi 1}\,\rho\,,\quad\delta Y_{\psi 1,y1,\phi 2b}=2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\,\rho\,,

\displaystyle-\delta Y_{\phi 1,y1,\psi 2c}=\delta Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2c}=\delta Y_{\psi 1,y1,\phi 2c}=-\delta Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2b}=\beta_{y1}\,\rho\,.

(247)

In (
235
)
Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,g}
,
g=\Phi 2A,\,\Phi 2B,\,Y2A,\,Y2B
, lead to four quadratic invariants
and from (
236
)
Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,Y2C}
to one more.

The various consistency conditions must be scheme invariant. We here check this by reducing their
variations to sums of
Y
’s which then show how they can be expressed in terms of quadratic invariants.
At lowest order the variations of (
210
), using (
224
), are just

\delta B_{1}=0\,,\quad\delta B_{2}=4\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}\,,\quad\delta B_{3}=0\,,\quad\delta B_{4}=2\,\beta_{y1}\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}\,,

(248)

and for the non planar conditions (
212
) at the next order from (
230a
)

\displaystyle\delta C_{1}=\delta C_{2}=\delta C_{3}=0\,,

\displaystyle\delta C_{4}=-\delta C_{5}=2\,Y{\!}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\psi 1,y1,y2f$}\,,\quad\delta C_{6}=-\delta C_{7}=2\,Y{\!}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle\phi 1,y1,y2f$}\,,

(249)

and for the variations of (
213
), (
214
), (
215
)

\displaystyle\delta D_{1}=\delta D_{2}=\delta D_{5}=\delta D_{7}=0\,,\quad\delta D_{3}=-2\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2a}\,,\quad\delta D_{4}=\delta D_{9}=-2\,Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2a}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{6}=-12\,Y_{\phi 1,\phi 2a,\lambda 1a}\,,\quad\delta D_{8}=-2\,Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2a}\,,\quad\delta D_{10}=2\,Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2a}+6\,Y_{\phi 2a,y1,\lambda 1b}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{11}=2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}Y_{\phi 1,\phi 2a,y1}-2\,\beta_{y1}Y_{\phi 1,\phi 2a,\psi 1}\,,\quad\delta D_{12}=-6\,\gamma_{\phi 2a}\,Y_{\psi 1,y1,\lambda 1b}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{13}=12\,\gamma_{\phi 1}\,Y_{\phi 2a,\psi 1,\lambda 1b}-12\,\beta_{\lambda 1b}\,Y_{\phi 1,\phi 2a,\psi 1}+4\,B_{1}\,X_{\phi 1,\psi 1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{14}=\gamma_{\phi 1}\,Y_{y1,y2a,\lambda 1a}-3\,\gamma_{\phi 2a}\,Y_{y1,\lambda 1a,\lambda 1b}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}+6\,\beta_{y1}\,Y_{\phi 2a,\lambda 1a,\lambda 1b}-2\,\beta_{y1}\,Y_{\phi 1,y2a,\lambda 1a}+2\,\beta_{y2a}\,Y_{\phi 1,y1,\lambda 1a}-B_{1}\,X_{y1,\lambda 1a}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{15}=0\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{16}=4\,\gamma_{\phi 1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,y1,y{2c}}+Y_{\phi 1,\psi{2c},y1}\big)+2\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,\phi 2b,y1}-2\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi{2a},y1}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}-4\,\gamma_{\phi 2c}\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}-2\,B_{2}\,X_{\phi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{17}=-4\,\gamma_{\phi 1}Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2d}+2\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,\phi 2c,y1}+Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2b}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{18}=4\,\gamma_{\phi 1}Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2c}+4\,\gamma_{\psi 1}Y_{\phi 1,\psi 2c,y1}-2\,\beta_{y1}\big(2\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 2b,y1}+Y_{\phi 2b,\psi 1,y1}\big)-2\,B_{2}\,X_{\psi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{19}=2\,\gamma_{\phi 1}\,Y_{\psi 1,\psi 2c,y1}+2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\,Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2c}+2\,\beta_{y1}\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 2b,y1}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}-2\big(\gamma_{\psi 1}(\beta_{y2d}-\beta_{y2e})+2\,\beta_{y1}\,\gamma_{\psi 2c}\big)\big/\beta_{y1}\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}+2\big(B_{3}\,X_{\phi 1,y1}+B_{4}\,X_{\psi 1,y1}\big)/\beta_{y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{20}=4\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2b}-Y_{\phi 2c,\psi 1,y1}\big)+2\,\beta_{y1}\big(2\,Y_{\psi 1,\psi 2a,y1}+Y_{\phi 2b,\psi 1,y1}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}-4\,\beta_{y2c}Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}+2\,B_{2}\,X_{\psi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{21}=2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(Y_{\psi 1,\psi 2c,y1}+Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2c}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{22}=-2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2d}+Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2e}\big)+2\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\psi 1,\psi 2c,y1}+Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2c}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{23}=4\,\gamma_{\phi 1}Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2d}-2\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2b}+Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2c}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{24}=2\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2d}-Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2e}\big)\,,\quad\delta D_{25}=4\,\gamma_{\psi 1}Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2e}-4\,\beta_{y1}Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2c}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{26}=2\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2d}-Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2e}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{27}=-8\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2d}+Y_{\phi 2c,\psi 1,y1}\big)-8\,\gamma_{\psi 1}Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2e}+4\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2c}-Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2b}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}+8(2\,\beta_{y2d}+\beta_{y2e})\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{28}=4\,\beta_{y1}Y_{\psi 1,\psi 2c,y1}-2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2d}+Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2e}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{29}=2\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2b}-Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2c}+2\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 2c,y1}\big)-2\,\gamma_{\phi 1}\big(3\,Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2d}+Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2e}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}+4\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\,Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2e}-4\,\beta_{y2d}\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{30}=2\,\beta_{y1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2d}+Y_{\phi 1,\phi 1,y2e}\big)+2\,(\beta_{y2d}+\beta_{y2e})\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{31}=4\,\beta_{y1}{\!}^{2}\,Y_{\psi 1,\psi 2b,y1}+2\,\beta_{y1}\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(Y_{\psi 1,\psi 2c,y1}+Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2c}\big)-2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}{\!}^{2}\,Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2e}+4\,B_{3}\,X_{\psi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{32}=-2\,\beta_{y1}{\!}^{2}\,Y_{\phi 2b,\psi 1,y1}-4\,\beta_{y1}\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2e}+Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2b}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}-2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\,(\beta_{y2d}-3\,\beta_{y2e})\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}-2\,B_{2}\,\beta_{y1}X_{\psi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{33}=-4\,\beta_{y1}{\!}^{2}\,Y_{\phi 2b,\psi 1,y1}+4\,\beta_{y1}\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,\psi 2c,y1}-Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2b}\big)+8\,\beta_{y1}\gamma_{\phi 1}\,Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2c}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}-4\,\gamma_{\psi 1}{\!}^{2}\,Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2e}+4\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\,(\beta_{y2d}+\beta_{y2e})\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}-4\,B_{2}\,\beta_{y1}\,X_{\psi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{34}=2\,\beta_{y1}{\!}^{2}\,\big(Y_{\phi 1,\psi 2b,y1}+Y_{\psi 1,\psi 2a,y1}\big)-2\,\beta_{y1}\gamma_{\phi 1}\,Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2c}-2\,\beta_{y1}\gamma_{\psi 1}\,Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2c}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}-2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}(3\,\beta_{y2d}-\beta_{y2e})\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}+2\,B_{3}\,X_{\phi 1,y1}+2\,B_{4}\,X_{\psi 1,y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta D_{35}=4\,\beta_{y1}{\!}^{2}\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 2a,y1}-2\,\beta_{y1}\gamma_{\phi 1}\,\big(Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2c}+Y_{\phi 2c,\psi 1,y1}\big)+4\,\beta_{y1}\gamma_{\psi 1}\,Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2b}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}-2\gamma_{\phi 1}{\!}^{2}\,\big(2\,Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2d}+Y_{\psi 1,y1,y2e}\big)+4\,\gamma_{\phi 1}\gamma_{\psi 1}\,Y_{\phi 1,y1,y2e}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt{}+2\,\big(\gamma_{\phi 1}\,\beta_{y2d}+\beta_{y1}\,\gamma_{\phi 2c}\big)\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y1}+2\,B_{4}\,X_{\phi 1,y1}\,.

(250)

For the constraints obtained in the reduced
{\mathcal{N}}=\frac{1}{2}
theory listed in sub section
9.1
, corresponding to a

\Phi^{3}
interaction, then with (
233
),
(
235
), (
236
),

\displaystyle\delta S_{0}=\delta S_{1}=\delta S_{2}=0\,,\quad\delta S_{3}=-2\,Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,Y2A}\,,\quad\delta S_{4}=-6\,Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,\Phi 2A}

\displaystyle\delta S_{5}=-4\,\beta_{Y1}\,Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,Y2A}+4\,\gamma_{\Phi 1}\,Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,Y2B}\,,

\displaystyle\delta S_{6}=-4\,\beta_{Y1}\,Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,\Phi 2A}+8\,\gamma_{\Phi 1}\,Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,\Phi 2B}+4\,S_{0}\,X_{\Phi 1,Y1}\,,

\displaystyle\delta S_{7}=8\,\gamma_{\Phi 1}\,\big(Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,Y2A}-Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,\Phi 2B}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\delta S_{8}=-4\,Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,Y2C}\,,\quad\delta S_{9}=2\,Y_{\Phi 1,Y1,Y2C}\,.

(251)

11
Conclusion

The detailed results given here for
\beta
-functions and anomalous dimensions
correspond to a
\overline{M\!S}
regularisation scheme. As is well known
attempting to extend supersymmetric theories away from their natural dimension
is problematic and generally inconsistent and these issues affect any variant of dimensional
regularisation
Siegel
;
Avdeev
;
Avdeev2
;
Stockinger
. For
{\mathcal{N}}=1

supersymmetry and scalar fermion theories, without gauge fields,
there are manifestly supersymmetric
regularisation schemes and potential problems with
\overline{M\!S}

arise only beyond three loops so long as the normalisation of fermion traces is
chosen appropriately.
These issues become significantly more severe for what we term
{\mathcal{N}}=\tfrac{1}{2}
symmetry in this
paper. Traces of three or more odd numbers of three dimensional Dirac gamma matrices
are potentially non zero due to the appearance of the three dimensional antisymmetric symbol. This is not
relevant for a fermion loop with three external scalars, due to momentum conservation,
but such contributions are present if a fermion loop has five external scalar lines. Of course analogous
problems with
\gamma_{5}
are present with perturbative calculations using
{\overline{M\!S}}
for chiral fermions.
Such problems also arise in four dimensional chiral gauge theories for loops with two external vector lines
and two external scalars and such loop diagrams contribute at four loops to 1PI contributions with two external
vector lines and also to the Yukawa
\beta
-function
Poole5
. In
Poole5
it was shown how
consistency with the
a
-function helps resolve some analogous
\gamma_{5}
issues.
In three dimensions similar potential problems arising for five vertex fermion loops
as sub graphs occur at four loops in the Yukawa vertex renormalisation where the relevant diagrams are of the form
