\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 57.31 42.89 C 57.31 43.98 56.43 44.85 55.35 44.85 C 54.27 44.85 53.39 43.98 53.39 42.89 C 53.39 41.81 54.27 40.94 55.35 40.94 C 56.43 40.94 57.31 41.81 57.31 42.89 Z M 55.35 42.89}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{40.00006pt}{31.00005pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ ,

(252)

together with various permutations of the internal vertices on the fermion loop.
Such diagrams are primitive since there are no subdivergences when evaluated in four
dimensions with some prescription for the contraction of two three dimensional
\epsilon
symbols.
A procedure for obtaining such contributions was described in
Zerf2
. However starting from four dimensional
Dirac or Majorana fermions contributions related to (
252
) are absent
Boyack
. The four dimensional
fermion splits into two three dimensional fermions whose Yukawa couplings have the opposite sign, as shown here
in appendix
A
.

In terms of the discussion of scheme changes and forming scheme invariants an alternative though
equivalent approach is obtained within the framework of the Hopf algebra approach
to Feynman diagrams
Connes1
;
Connes2
;
Panzer
.
The requirement of scheme invariance is identical with finding linear sums of graphs
such that the Hopf algebra coproduct is cocommutative. A potentially interesting possibility
is whether there is any extension of the Hopf algebraic approach to deriving consistency conditions, such as those
considered here in section 9, which might avoid some of the rather tortuous analysis required
here and in
Poole:2019kcm
.

The results obtained here suggest that there are potentially many interesting fixed points in scalar fermion
theories once more than three scalar fields are allowed and the condition that there is just a single
Yukawa coupling is relaxed. Finding a large
n_{f}
expansion for such theories may be tractable.

12
Note Added, Fixed Points with One Scalar or One Fermion Field

Since this paper was finished Pannell and Stergiou
StergiouF
have investigated in detail possible fixed
points in fermion scalar theories with low numbers of scalars and fermions. Many possiblities were discovered.
Here we illustrate some results for either one scalar or one fermion which can be obtained quite easily.

For a single scalar field and
n
two component real fermions the couplings are just
\lambda
and

y
a real symmetric
n\times n
matrix. For four dimensional Majorana fermions
n
should be even.
At one loop order the
\beta
-functions in
4-\varepsilon
dimensions reduce,
with the usual rescaling to eliminate factors of
4\pi
, to

\displaystyle\beta_{y}={}

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon\,y+3\,y^{3}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{2})\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda}={}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\,\lambda+3\,\lambda^{2}+2\,\lambda\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{2})-12\,\mathrm{tr}(y^{4})\,.

(253)

To solve
\beta_{y}=0,\ \beta_{\lambda}=0
we set
\varepsilon=1
and diagonalise
y
by an
O(n)
transformation
so that it has diagonal elements
y_{i},\,i=1,\dots,n
. The Yukawa
\beta
-function then gives

y_{i}=6\,y_{i}{\!}^{3}+y_{i}\,R\,,\ \ R={\tilde{s}\sum_{i}}\,y_{i}{\!}^{2}\,,\quad\Rightarrow\quad y_{i}{\!}^{2}=\frac{1}{n+6}\,,\ \ R=\frac{n}{n+6}\,.

(254)

Substituting in
\beta_{\lambda}=0
then gives

\lambda=\frac{1}{6(n+6)}\Big(6-n\pm\sqrt{n^{2}+132\,n+36}\,\Big)\,.

(255)

The stability matrix becomes

M=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\lambda}{\beta}_{\lambda}&\partial_{\lambda}\beta_{y_{j}}\\
\partial_{y_{i}}\beta_{\lambda}&\partial_{y_{i}}{\beta}_{y_{j}}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(256)

where
y_{i},\lambda
are determined by (
254
), (
255
). Without any loss of generality we can take

y_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n+6}}\,s_{i}\,,\qquad s_{i}=\begin{cases}1\,,\quad&i=1,\dots p,\\
-1,\,&i=p+1,\dots,n\end{cases}\,,\quad p=0,\dots,n\,.

(257)

Then
M
becomes for the two possible solutions for
\lambda
in (
255
)

M=\frac{1}{n+6}\begin{pmatrix}\pm\sqrt{n^{2}+132\,n+36}&0\\
*&6\,\delta_{ij}+s_{i}s_{j}\ \end{pmatrix}\,.

(258)

The eigenvalues are then

\pm\tfrac{1}{n+6}\sqrt{n^{2}+132\,n+36}\,,\quad 1\,,\quad\tfrac{6}{n+6}\ \mbox{degeneracy}\ n-1\,,

(259)

where the eigenvectors for the last two cases can be given by

v_{i}=s_{i}\,,\quad v_{1}=1,\,v_{i}=\begin{cases}-1,\,\ i=2,\dots,p\\
1,\,\ i=p+1,\dots n\end{cases},\ v_{j}=0\,,j\neq 1,i\,.

(260)

The fixed points corresponding to (
257
) are invariant under
O(p)\times O(n-p)
. Each
point on the orbit corresponding to the coset
O(n)/O(p)\times O(n-p)
, of dimension
p(n-p)
,
generated by the action of
O(n)
defines an equivalent theory.

For one fermion and
n_{s}=m+1
scalars then by an
O(n_{s})
rotation the Yukawa interaction can be considered to
involve just one scalar
\sigma
while the remaining
m
scalars
\varphi_{a}
correspond to a purely scalar theory
formed by quartic polynomial in
\varphi
together
with interactions involving
\sigma
. If the maximal
O(m)
symmetry is preserved then there are three couplings

\lambda_{1}
corresponding the
O(m)
invariant quartic
(\varphi^{2})^{2}
,
\lambda_{2}
for
\sigma^{4}
and
g

for a
\varphi^{2}\sigma^{2}
interaction.
For
g=0
there are two decoupled theories. The resulting lowest order
\beta
-functions
take the form

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda_{1}}={}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\,\lambda_{1}+(m+8)\lambda_{1}^{2}+g^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{\lambda_{2}}={}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\,\lambda_{2}+9\,\lambda_{2}^{2}+m\,g^{2}+2\,y^{2}\lambda_{2}-4\,y^{4}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{g}={}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\,g+\big((m+2)\lambda_{1}+3\,\lambda_{2}\big)g+4\,g^{2}+y^{2}g\,.

(261)

For
y=0
this is just a biconical theory, for
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=g
there is an
O(m+1)
symmetry. Other
fixed points with
g
non zero are irrational and have two quadratic invariants,
The Yukawa
\beta
-function gives at a fixed point
y^{2}=\frac{1}{7}
. For
m=4,\,\varepsilon=1
there is a rational solution

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{21},\,\lambda_{2}=0,\,g=\frac{1}{7}
, otherwise the
g\neq 0
solutions are irrational.
For the scalar invariants

S=3m(m+2)\,\lambda_{1}{\!}^{2}+9\,\lambda_{2}{\!}^{2}+m\,g^{2}\,,\qquad a_{0}=m(m+2)\,\lambda_{1}+3\,\lambda_{2}+2\,m\,g\,.

(262)

For the first few values of
m
these take the values, with
\varepsilon=1
and
g,y
non zero,

m

S

a_{0}

0

\tfrac{9}{49}

\tfrac{3}{7}

1

0.29635

0.23347

0.8081

0.6518

2

0.42391

0.28682

1.2569

0.9066

3

0.55581

0.34503

1.7530

1.1996

4

0.68552

0.41049

0.66475

\tfrac{32}{49}

2.2841

1.5422

2.3582

\tfrac{16}{7}

Note that
\tfrac{32}{49}\approx 0.65306
.
As a function of
m
,
S
has the form

There are bifurcation points close to
m=4,6
where two fixed points are created or annihilated.

At lowest order the stability matrix eigenvalues can be determined from the
3\times 3

matrix determined from
\beta_{\lambda_{1}},\,\beta_{\lambda_{2}},\beta_{g}
analogously to (
256
).
For
m=1,2
the solution corresponding to the first case in (
) has three positive eigenvalues and is
therefore RG stable.
For
m=4
the two additional solutions each have a small eigenvalue of opposite sign.
These both tend to zero as
m
approaches the bifurcation point,
m\approx 3.965
.

For
m\geq 2
there are scalar theories with reduced symmetry which should lead to a ranged of
additional fixed points with a Yukawa coupling to a single fermion.

Appendix A
Majorana Fermions and Their Reduction

For a spinor field
\Psi
its conjugate
\bar{\Psi}
is defined by

\bar{\Psi}=\Psi^{\dagger}A\,,

(270)

where
A
satisfies (the choice of both signs is a matter of convention, they are chosen here for later convenience)

A\,\gamma^{\mu}A^{-1}=-(\gamma^{\mu})^{\dagger}\,,\qquad A^{\dagger}=-A\,,

(271)

with, for
d=4
, the
4\times 4
Dirac matrices here defined by

\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=2\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\,\mathds{1}\,,\qquad\eta^{\mu\nu}={\rm diag.}(-1,1,1,1)\,.

(272)

Taking
\gamma_{5}=i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}
, then
A\gamma_{5}A^{-1}=-\gamma_{5}{}^{\dagger}

and
\gamma_{5}{}^{2}=\mathds{1}
.
Under a reflection in the
x^{1}x^{2}
plane, charge conjugation and time reversal

\displaystyle\Psi\longrightarrow\hskip-17.07182pt{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}_{3}$}}\hskip 11.38092pt\Psi_{r_{3}}=R\,\Psi\big|_{x^{3}\to-x^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle{\bar{\Psi}}\longrightarrow\hskip-17.07182pt{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{R}_{3}$}}\hskip 11.38092pt{\bar{\Psi}}_{r_{3}}={\bar{\Psi}}R^{-1}\big|_{x^{3}\to-x^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle\Psi\longrightarrow\hskip-17.07182pt{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt\Psi_{c}=C\,{\bar{\Psi}}^{T}\,,

\displaystyle{\bar{\Psi}}\longrightarrow\hskip-17.07182pt{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{C}$}}\hskip 14.22636pt{\bar{\Psi}}_{c}=-{\Psi}^{T}C^{-1}\,,

\displaystyle\Psi\longrightarrow\hskip-17.07182pt{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{T}$}}\hskip 11.38092pt\Psi_{t}=T\,\Psi\big|_{x^{0}\to-x^{0}}\,,

\displaystyle{\bar{\Psi}}\longrightarrow\hskip-17.07182pt{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{T}$}}\hskip 11.38092pt{\bar{\Psi}}_{t}=-{\bar{\Psi}}\,T^{-1}\big|_{x^{0}\to-x^{0}}\,,

(273)

where
\mathcal{T}
is antilinear and

R^{-1}\gamma^{\mu}\,R=\begin{cases}\gamma^{\mu},\ &\mu=0,1,2\\
-\gamma^{3}\,&\mu=3\end{cases},\quad C^{-1}\gamma^{\mu}\,C=-(\gamma^{\mu})^{T}\,,\quad T^{-1}\gamma^{\mu}{}^{*}\,T=\begin{cases}\gamma^{\mu},\ &\mu=1,2,3\\
-\gamma^{0}\,&\mu=0\end{cases},

(274)

with
C^{-1}\gamma_{5}\,C=\gamma_{5}{\!}^{T},\ T^{-1}\gamma_{5}{}^{*}\,T=\gamma_{5}
.
In general

C^{T}=-C\,,\qquad A^{*}C^{\dagger}A=-C^{-1}\,,\qquad A^{-1}T^{\dagger}A=-T^{-1}\,,\qquad A^{-1}R^{\dagger}A=R^{-1}\,.

(275)

Using these results
R,T
can be given by

R=i\,\gamma^{3}\gamma_{5}\,,\qquad T=C^{\dagger}A\,\gamma^{0}\gamma_{5}\quad\Rightarrow\quad TT^{*}=-\mathds{1}\,,

(276)

and
\Psi\longrightarrow\hskip-21.33955pt{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{R}}_{3}{\!}^{2}$}}\hskip 8.5359ptR^{2}\Psi=\Psi,\ \Psi\longrightarrow\hskip-18.49411pt{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}^{2}$}}\hskip 8.5359pt-CC^{-1}{}^{T}\Psi=\Psi,\ \Psi\longrightarrow\hskip-18.49411pt{\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}}^{2}$}}\hskip 8.5359ptTT^{*}\Psi=-\Psi
.

For a Majorana fermion

\Psi=\Psi_{c}\,,

(277)

and for present purposes we consider the Lagrangian

{\cal L}_{M}=-i\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\bar{\Psi}}\,\gamma\cdot\partial\,\Psi-i\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\bar{\Psi}}\,{\mathcal{M}}\,\Psi-i\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\bar{\Psi}}\,{\mathcal{Y}}^{a}\,\Psi\,\phi^{a}\,,

(278)

with both
{\mathcal{M}},\,{\mathcal{Y}^{a}}
real, symmetric and
[{\mathcal{M}},{\gamma}^{\mu}]=[{\mathcal{Y}^{a},{\gamma}^{\mu}}]=0
. With the conventions (
270
) and (
271
)

{\cal L}_{M}{}^{\dagger}={\cal L}_{M}
.

For reduction to three dimensions a convenient basis is obtained by taking
\gamma^{\mu}\to{\tilde{\gamma}}^{\mu}

with, adapting
Kubota
;
Boyack
;
archipelago
,

{\tilde{\gamma}}^{\mu}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu}&0\\
0&-\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu}\end{pmatrix}\,,\ \mu=0,1,2\,,\quad\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu}=(i\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},-\sigma_{1})\,,\quad{\tilde{\gamma}}^{3}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(279)

Here
{\tilde{\sigma}}^{\mu},\ -{\tilde{\sigma}}^{\mu}
correspond to the two inequivalent two dimensional irreducible
representations for the
d=3
Dirac algebra.
\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu}i\sigma_{2}=(-\mathds{1}_{2},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3})
form
a basis for symmetric
2\times 2
matrices.
For the
d=4
representation defined by (
279
)

\tau={\tilde{\gamma}}^{0}{\tilde{\gamma}}^{1}{\tilde{\gamma}}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad{\tilde{\gamma}}_{5}=i{\tilde{\gamma}}^{0}{\tilde{\gamma}}^{1}{\tilde{\gamma}}^{2}{\tilde{\gamma}}^{3}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(280)

and

\displaystyle A={\tilde{\gamma}}^{0}=\begin{pmatrix}i\sigma_{2}&0\\
0&-i\sigma_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 39.83368ptR=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle C=i\,{\tilde{\gamma}}^{0}{\tilde{\gamma}}^{3}{\tilde{\gamma}_{5}}=\begin{pmatrix}i\sigma_{2}&0\\
0&i\sigma_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad T=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\sigma_{2}\\
i\sigma_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(281)

The representation in (
279
) can be related to the more commonplace chiral representation by

U\,{\tilde{\gamma}}^{\mu}\,U^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\,\sigma^{\mu}\\
i\,{\bar{\sigma}}^{\mu}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad U\,{\tilde{\gamma}}_{5}\,U^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\sigma^{\mu}=(1,\,{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})\,,\ \ {\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}}=(1,\,-{\boldsymbol{\sigma}})\,,

(282)

where

U=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{3}-\sigma_{2}&\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3}\\
\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}-1&\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}-1\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad U^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{3}-\sigma_{2}&\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}-1\\
\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3}&\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}-1\end{pmatrix}\,.

(283)

With (
279
) the spinor field decomposes into two
d=3
two-component spinors as

\Psi=\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{1}\\
\psi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad{\bar{\Psi}}=({\bar{\psi}_{1}},-{\bar{\psi}}_{2})\,,\quad{\bar{\psi}_{a}}=\psi_{a}{\!}^{\dagger}i\sigma_{2}\,,\quad\psi_{1}=\psi_{1}{\!}^{*}\,,\ \ \psi_{2}=-\psi_{2}{\!}^{*}\,.

(284)

Using the decomposition (
284
), and taking

{\mathcal{M}}\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}m&0\\
0&m\end{smallmatrix}\right),\ {\mathcal{Y}^{a}}\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y^{a}&0\\
0&y^{a}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, (
278
) becomes,

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{M}={}

\displaystyle-i\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\textstyle\sum_{a=1,2}}\,{\bar{\psi}}_{a}\,\tilde{\sigma}\cdot\partial\,\psi_{a}-\tfrac{1}{2}({\bar{\psi}}_{1}\hskip 0.8pt\partial_{3}\hskip 0.5pt\psi_{2}+{\bar{\psi}}_{2}\hskip 0.8pt\partial_{3}\hskip 0.5pt\psi_{1})

\displaystyle{}-i\,\tfrac{1}{2}({\bar{\psi}}_{1}\hskip 0.5ptm\hskip 0.5pt\psi_{1}-{\bar{\psi}}_{2}\hskip 0.5ptm\hskip 0.5pt\psi_{2})-i\,\tfrac{1}{2}({\bar{\psi}}_{1}\,y^{a}\psi_{1}-{\bar{\psi}}_{2}\,y^{a}\psi_{2})\phi^{a}\,.

(285)

For zero mass,
m=0
, this has a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry where
\psi_{1}\leftrightarrow\psi_{2},\,\phi^{a}\to-\phi^{a}
.
This ensures the cancellation of fermion loops with odd numbers of Yukawa vertices.
For a symmetry
h^{-1}y^{a}h=y^{a},\ h^{-1}\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu}h=\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu}
with
h\in H_{M}\subset O(n_{f})
then in general
there is a symmetry
H_{M}
but this extends to
(H_{M}\times H_{M})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
when
d=3
.
For a single scalar this becomes
(O(n_{f})\times O(n_{f}))\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
when
m=0
.

With conventions from (
273
)

\displaystyle(\psi_{1r_{3}},\,\psi_{2r_{3}})=(\psi_{1},-\psi_{2})\big|_{x^{3}\to-x^{3}}\ ,\qquad(\bar{\psi}_{1r_{3}},\,\bar{\psi}_{2r_{3}})=(\bar{\psi}_{1},-\bar{\psi}_{2})\big|_{x^{3}\to-x^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle(\psi_{1t},\,\psi_{2t})=(i\sigma_{2}\psi_{2},i\sigma_{2}\psi_{1})\big|_{x^{0}\to-x^{0}}\ ,\quad(\bar{\psi}_{1t},\,\bar{\psi}_{2t})=(-\bar{\psi}_{2}i\sigma_{2},-\bar{\psi}_{1}i\sigma_{2})\big|_{x^{0}\to-x^{0}}\,.

(286)

Thus (
285
) is invariant under
{\mathcal{R}}_{3},{\mathcal{T}}

so long as the time reversal
\mathcal{T}
transformation is combined with
\psi_{1}\leftrightarrow\psi_{2}
,

i\,{\bar{\psi}}_{1}\psi_{1}\longrightarrow\hskip-17.07182pt{\raisebox{-5.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{T}$}}\hskip 8.5359pt-i\,{\bar{\psi}}_{2t}\psi_{2t}
. If
\mathcal{T}
is combined with
\psi_{1}\leftrightarrow\psi_{2}
the sign of the mass term
is reversed.
However for a reflections
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
or
{\mathcal{R}}_{2}
, corresponding to
x^{1}\to-x^{1}
or
x^{2}\to-x^{2}
,
then instead

{\bar{\psi}}_{1}\psi_{1}\leftrightarrow-{\bar{\psi}}_{2}\psi_{2}
and the individual mass terms are not invariant
by themselves under either
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
or
{\mathcal{R}}_{2}
, as expected for three dimensional spinors.
The three dimensional theory with just one two component spinor cannot have mass terms which preserve

{\mathcal{T}}
or
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
invariance
Witten
.

If the Yukawa interaction is modified to

{\cal L}_{Y}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\bar{\Psi}}\,{\tilde{\gamma}}_{3}{\tilde{\gamma}}_{5}\,{\mathcal{Y}}^{a}\Psi\,\phi^{a}=-i\,\tfrac{1}{2}({\bar{\psi}}_{1}\,y^{a}\psi_{1}+{\bar{\psi}}_{2}\,y^{a}\psi_{2})\phi^{a}\,,

(287)

it is then necessary to include the four loop diagrams corresponding to (
252
).
the symmetry for
d=3
is enhanced to a subgroup of
O(2n_{f})
, for
n_{s}=1
the symmetry is
O(2n_{f})
.
This prescription allows for fermion loops with odd numbers of Yukawa vertices but is not relevant up to three loops.
By including contributions corresponding to diagrams of the form (
284
) it was implicitly followed in

Zerf2
in their
four loop calculation. Nevertheless (
287
) breaks Lorentz invariance for
d\neq 3
though
O(2,1)

is preserved. Applying dimensional
regularisation,
d=4-\varepsilon
, the one loop counterterms necessary when starting from (
278
)
or equivalently (
285
) are, for a single scalar
\sigma
,

{\cal L}_{\rm ct}^{(1)}=\frac{y^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}\varepsilon}\Big(i\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\bar{\Psi}}\,\gamma\cdot\partial\,\Psi-i\,m\,{\bar{\Psi}}\Psi+(\partial\sigma)^{2}+6\,m^{2}\sigma^{2}-i\,y\,{\bar{\Psi}}\Psi\,\sigma+\tfrac{1}{3}\,y^{2}\,\sigma^{4}\Big)\,.

(288)

For the modified Yukawa interaction (
287
) the result becomes

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm ct}^{(1)}\big|_{\rm modified}\!\!={}

\displaystyle\frac{y^{2}}{(4\pi)^{2}\varepsilon}\Big(i\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\bar{\Psi}}\,\gamma\cdot\partial\,\Psi-i\,{\bar{\Psi}}\,\gamma_{3}\partial_{3}\,\Psi-i\,m\,{\bar{\Psi}}\Psi+\tfrac{2}{3}\big((\partial\sigma)^{2}-(\partial_{3}\sigma)^{2}\big){}+4\,m^{2}\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt{}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y\,{\bar{\Psi}}\gamma_{3}\gamma_{5}\Psi\,\sigma+\tfrac{1}{12}\,y^{2}\,\sigma^{4}\Big)\,.

(289)

Besides breaking Lorentz invariance explicitly in the kinetic terms the
counterterm has different coefficients for the Yukawa and the quartic scalar terms.
For a consistent flow it would be necessary to
allow for modified kinetic terms for the scalar and fermion fields so that the propagation
velocity is different in the 3-direction from the 1,2 directions, bringing in two new parameters consistent
with the breaking
O(3,1)
to
O(2,1)
. Whether the
\varepsilon
-expansion can be applied in this case is unclear.

An alternative possibilty, yet to be explored, is
to take
{\mathcal{M}}\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}m&0\\
0&M\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and require

\Lambda\gg M\gg m
for
\Lambda
some cutoff.
This breaks Lorentz invariance more softly and should lead to
\psi_{2}
being decoupled so as to generate
an effective theory for
\psi_{1}
.
This non Lorentz invariant theory can potentially be extended to
{\mathcal{N}}=\frac{1}{2}
supersymmetry away from
d=3
.

Appendix B
Algebra of
d
and
w
Tensors

The tensors defined by (
71
) and (
78
) satisfy identities which allow determination of
eigenvalues,

\displaystyle d^{abef}\,d^{cdef}=\tfrac{1}{n_{s}-1}\,a\big(\tfrac{1}{2}n_{s}(\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc})-\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}\big)+e\,w^{abcd}+b\,d^{abcd}\,,

\displaystyle d^{abef}\,w^{cdef}=w^{abef}\,d^{cdef}=f\,w^{abcd}+h\,d^{abcd}\,,

\displaystyle w^{abef}\,w^{cdef}=\tfrac{1}{n_{s}-1}\,a^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}n_{s}(\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc})-\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}\big)+e^{\prime}\,w^{abcd}+b^{\prime}\,d^{abcd}\,,

(290)

where
a,b
are as in (
73
). For consistency

e\,a^{\prime}=h\,a\,,\quad\ f\,a^{\prime}=b^{\prime}\,a\,,\quad f\,h=b^{\prime}\,e\,,\quad\tfrac{n_{s}}{n_{s}-1}\,a=f^{2}+e\,h-b\,f-e\,e^{\prime}\,.

(291)

The relevant eigenvalue equations necessary for obtaining the anomalous dimensions
\phi^{2}
operators
given general perturbative results are

d^{abcd}\,v^{cd}=\mu\,v^{ab}\,,\qquad w^{abcd}\,v^{cd}=\nu\,v^{ab}\,\,,

(292)

for symmetric traceless
v^{ab}
. (
318
) then requires

\mu^{2}=e\,\nu+b\,\mu+\tfrac{n_{s}}{n_{s}-1}\,a\,,\quad\mu\,\nu=f\,\nu+h\,\mu\,,\quad\nu^{2}=e^{\prime}\,\nu+b^{\prime}\,\mu+\tfrac{n_{s}}{n_{s}-1}\,a^{\prime}\,.

(293)

As a consequence of (
291
) the last equation is redundant and then
eliminating
\nu
leads to a cubic equation for
\mu
whose solutions determine
\nu
.
There are thus three possibilities
\mu_{i},\,\nu_{i}
. The associated degeneracies are then determined
by

{\textstyle\sum_{i}}\,d_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}(n_{s}-1)(n_{s}+2)\,,\qquad{\textstyle\sum_{i}}\,d_{i}\,\mu_{i}={\textstyle\sum_{i}}\,d_{i}\,\nu_{i}=0\,.

(294)

Appendix D
U(1)
Scalar Fermion Theory Consistency Equations

The derivation of consistency relations for the three loop Yukawa couplings can be illustrated by
restricting to case when
U(1)
symmetry is imposed. The number of couplings is significantly
reduced but there remain non trivial relations which are a subset of the general case
7
7

7

The consistency
relations in this case were discussed less completely in
Jack:2013sha
.

At lowest order

T_{IJ}{\!}^{(2)}\,{\rm d}g^{I}{\rm d}^{\prime}g^{J}=t_{2}\big(\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}y^{i}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}{\bar{y}}_{i})+\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}y^{i})\big)\,,

(330)

and

{A}^{(3)}=a_{3a}\,\big(\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{j})+\mathrm{tr}(y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{j})\big)+a_{3b}\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j})\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{i})\,.

(331)

The consistency equations are then

a_{3a}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,\qquad a_{3b}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{2}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,.

(332)

At the next order

\displaystyle T_{IJ}{\!}^{(3)}\,{\rm d}g^{I}{\rm d}^{\prime}g^{J}\big|_{{\rm d}\lambda}={}

\displaystyle t_{3\lambda}\,{\rm d}\lambda_{ij}{}^{kl}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}\lambda_{kl}{}^{ij}

\displaystyle T_{IJ}{\!}^{(3)}\,{\rm d}g^{I}{\rm d}^{\prime}g^{J}\big|_{{\rm d}{\bar{y}}}={}

\displaystyle t_{3a_{1}}\,\big(\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}y^{i}\,{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{j})+\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}y^{i})\big)

\displaystyle{}+t_{3a_{2}}\,\big(\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}y^{j}\,{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{i})+\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{i}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}y^{j})\big)

\displaystyle{}+t_{3a_{3}}\,\big(\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{i}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{j})+\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}{\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{i})\big)

\displaystyle{}+t_{3b_{1}}\,\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}y^{j})\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{j}\,y^{i})+t_{3b_{2}}\,\mathrm{tr}({\rm d}{\bar{y}}_{i}\,y^{j})\,\mathrm{tr}({\bar{y}}_{j}\,{\rm d}^{\prime}y^{i})

\displaystyle a_{5n}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{2}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}+(t_{3b_{2}}+t_{3b_{3}})\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+(t_{4e_{11}}+t_{4e_{15}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}=t_{3a_{1}}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+t_{4e_{1}}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}+(t_{4f_{7}}+t_{4f_{8}})\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle t_{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+(t_{3a_{2}}+t_{3a_{3}})\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a}+(t_{4f_{2}}+t_{4f_{4}})\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle t_{3b_{1}}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+(t_{4e_{6}}+t_{4e_{9}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}+t_{4f_{3}}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,

\displaystyle a_{5o}={}

\displaystyle t_{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}+(t_{4e_{2}}+t_{4e_{4}})\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}+(t_{4f_{1}}+t_{4f_{5}})\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}=2\,t_{4e_{7}}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}+2\,t_{4f_{6}}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle t_{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3h}+2(t_{4e_{12}}+t_{4e_{14}})\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,

\displaystyle a_{5p}={}

\displaystyle t_{2}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3m}+(t_{3b_{2}}+t_{3b_{3}})\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}=t_{2}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3s}+(t_{4g_{1}}+t_{4g_{3}})\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}

\displaystyle a_{5q}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{8}(t_{2}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3w}+t_{4a_{2}}\,\beta_{\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt}1b})\,,

\displaystyle a_{5r}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\,t_{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}+\tfrac{1}{4}\,(t_{3a_{2}}+t_{3a_{3}})\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+\tfrac{1}{2}(t_{4e_{3}}+t_{4e_{5}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\,t_{3a_{1}}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+\tfrac{1}{2}(t_{4e_{8}}+t_{4e_{10}}+t_{4e_{13}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,

\displaystyle a_{5s}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}(t_{4d_{1}}+t_{4d_{2}}+t_{4d_{3}}+t_{4d_{4}}+t_{4d_{5}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,

\displaystyle a_{5t}={}

\displaystyle t_{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3i}+(t_{4f_{1}}+t_{4f_{2}}+t_{4f_{4}}+t_{4f_{5}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}=(t_{4d_{1}}+2\,t_{4f_{6}}+t_{4f_{7}}+t_{4f_{8}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(t_{3a_{1}}+t_{3a_{3}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+(t_{4d_{2}}+t_{4d_{4}}+t_{4f_{1}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle t_{3a_{2}}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+(t_{4d_{3}}+t_{4d_{5}}+t_{4f_{3}}+t_{4f_{5}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,

\displaystyle a_{5u}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}+\tfrac{1}{2}(t_{3a_{2}}+t_{3a_{3}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+\tfrac{1}{2}(t_{4f_{2}}+t_{4f_{4}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{3a_{1}}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}+\tfrac{1}{2}(t_{4f_{3}}+t_{4f_{7}}+t_{4f_{8}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,

\displaystyle a_{5v}={}

\displaystyle t_{2}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3{\tilde{o}}}+(t_{4g_{1}}+t_{4g_{3}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}=t_{2}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3{\tilde{p}}}+(t_{4g_{2}}+t_{4g_{3}})\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle t_{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3p}+(t_{3a_{2}}+t_{3a_{3}})\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}=t_{3a_{1}}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}+t_{4g_{1}}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,,

\displaystyle a_{5w}={}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\,t_{2}\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3{\tilde{z}}}\,.

(352)

This leads to the non planar condition from the relations involving
a_{5p},\,a_{5v}

U_{10}=2\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}3s}-\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3m})-2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}(\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}{\tilde{o}}}-\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3p})+(\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}-2\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a})\,\beta_{y{\hskip 0.5pt}2f}\,,

(353)

and just two other conditions

\displaystyle U_{11}=\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}(\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}-2\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3i})+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}(\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3f}-\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3g}-\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3h})+(\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}-2\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a})\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}\,,

\displaystyle U_{12}=2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}{\!}^{2}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3j}-\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}{\!}^{2}(2\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}3e}+\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3g})+\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}{\!}^{3}\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}3d}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt{}-(2\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}-\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b})(\gamma_{\phi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2b}-\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}1}\,\gamma_{\psi{\hskip 0.5pt}2a})\,.

(354)

The results obtained in (
338
), (
347
), (
353
), (
354
)
are a subset of those obtained in section
291
in the general case. We list some scheme variations
which are not immediately evident from previous results

\displaystyle\delta U_{6}={}

\displaystyle-2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\,Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2a}\,,\qquad\delta U_{8}=-2\,\gamma_{\psi 1}\big(Y_{\phi 1,\psi 1,y2a}+3\,Y_{\phi 2a,\psi 1,y1}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\delta U_{9}={}

(355)

Appendix E
Scheme Variations in General

The coordinates in quantum field theories are the couplings. Physical results should be
invariant under reparametrisations of the couplings or in this context changes of renormalisation scheme.
Of course determining possible invariants is an exercise in differential geometry. Under changes
of scale there is a RG flow in the space of couplings determined by a vector field, the
\beta
-function,
and at any fixed point where the
\beta
-function vanishes
the scale dimensions of operators are determined by the eigenvalues of
the anomalous dimension matrix which, at a fixed point, is a two index tensor under reparametrisations.

In a perturbative context the possible reparametrisations of couplings
are naturally restricted to preserve the form
of the
\beta
-function in terms of contributions corresponding to 1PI diagrams which are superficially
divergent. For an expansion in terms of diagrams with increasing loop order there a usually a restricted set of
possible vertices
\{V_{\rm v}\}
, labelled by
{\rm v}
and edges
\{E_{\rm e}\}
labelled by
{\rm e}
.
The various possible
{\rm e}
correspond to the different fields in the theory and
each
{\rm v}
to the different basic couplings.
For
n_{\rm v}
lines meeting at a particular vertex
V_{\rm v}
there is then an associated coupling

(G^{\rm v})_{i_{1}\dots i_{n_{\rm v}}}
where
i_{r}
an index associated with the a diagram line or edge,

{\rm e}
, connected to the vertex
{\rm v}
.
In a diagram with a line
{\rm e}
there is an associated two index link or propagator

(P^{\rm e})_{ij}
with
i,j=1,\dots n_{\rm e}
.
For each coupling
G^{\rm v}
there there is a symmetry group
{\sl G}_{\rm v}\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{n_{\rm v}}

generated by permutations of those lines corresponding to identical particles.
For convenience we may consider a basis in which the couplings are real and
(P^{\rm e})_{ij}
is symmetric, otherwise
for complex couplings they form conjugate pairs. For simplicity we restrict to dimensionless couplings.

It is convenient to adopt a notation where for any set of
\{(R^{\rm e})_{ij}\}
and

\{(\kappa^{\rm e})_{ij}\}

\displaystyle(G^{\rm v}\circ R)_{i_{1}\dots i_{n_{\rm v}}}={}

\displaystyle(G^{\rm v})_{j_{1}\dots j_{n_{\rm v}}}\,(R^{1})_{j_{1}i_{1}}\dots(R^{n_{\rm v}})_{j_{n_{\rm v}}i_{n_{\rm v}}}\,,

\displaystyle(G^{\rm v}\kappa)_{i_{1}\dots i_{n_{\rm v}}}={}

\displaystyle{\textstyle\sum_{r}}\,(G^{\rm v})_{i_{1}\dots i_{r{-1}}j\hskip 0.5pti_{r{+1}}\dots i_{n_{\rm v}}}\,(\kappa^{r})_{ji_{r}}\,.

(356)

Clearly
(G^{\rm v}\circ R)\circ R^{\prime}=G^{\rm v}\circ R\hskip 0.5ptR^{\prime}
, with
R\hskip 0.5ptR^{\prime}=\{R_{\rm e}R^{\prime}{\!}_{\rm e}\}
,

((G^{\rm v}\,\kappa)\,\kappa^{\prime})-((G^{\rm v}\,\kappa^{\prime})\,\kappa)=(G^{\rm v}\,[\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}])
.
For an overall symmetry
{\cal G}
then
G^{\rm v}\circ R=G^{\rm v}
,
for each
R^{\rm e}
belonging to the appropriate representation
of
{\cal G}
, and
(P^{\rm e})_{kl}(R^{\rm e})_{ki}(R^{\rm e})_{lj}=(P^{\rm e})_{ij}
. For a vacuum diagram there
is a corresponding amplitude formed by joining couplings for each vertex with appropriate propagators

A(G,P)=A(g)\,,\qquad g^{\rm v}=G^{\rm v}\circ P^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,\qquad A(g)=A(g,\mathds{1})\,,

(357)

with
G=\{G^{\rm v}\},\ P=\{P_{\rm e}\}
and
P_{\rm e}{}^{\frac{1}{2}}
is required to be symmetric. In general we require

A(G\circ{\cal R},{\cal R}^{T}P\,{\cal R})=A(G,P)\,,\quad A(g\circ{\cal R})=A(g)\,,\qquad{\cal R}=\{{\cal R}^{\rm e}\}\,,\quad{\cal R}^{\rm e}{\cal R}^{\rm e}{}^{T}=\mathds{1}^{\rm e}\,.

(358)

Reparametrisations of relevance here are generated by

\displaystyle{\cal D}_{v,w}(G,P)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\rm v}v^{\rm v}(G,P)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial G^{\rm v}}+2\,\sum_{\rm e}\big(P^{\rm e}\,w^{\rm e}(G,P)P^{\rm e}\big)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial P^{\rm e}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\rm v}v^{\rm v}(G,P)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial G^{\rm v}}-2\,\sum_{\rm e}w^{\rm e}(G,P)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial P^{\rm e}{}^{-1}}\,,

(359)

where
v^{\rm v}(G,P),\,(w^{\rm e}(G,P))_{ij}
are determined in terms of sums of 1PI one and higher loop
vertex and propagator graphs with vertices mapped to the appropriate
G^{\rm v}
and similarly internal lines to
P^{\rm e}
.
The 2 in (
359
) is introduced for later convenience.
For a finite transformation

G^{\rm v}\to G^{\rm v}{}^{\prime},\,P^{\rm e}\to P^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}
then

G^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(G,P)=\exp\big({\cal D}_{v,w}(G,P)\big)\,G^{\rm v}\,,\qquad P^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(G,P)=\exp\big({\cal D}_{v,w}(G,P)\big)\,P^{\rm e}\,.

(360)

With this definition

G^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(G,P)=G^{\rm v}+f^{\rm v}(G,P)\,,\qquad P^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(G,P)^{-1}=P^{\rm e}{}^{-1}-2\,c^{\rm e}(G,P)\,,

(361)

where
f^{\rm v},\,c^{\rm e}
can be expressed in terms of contributions from 1PI vertex and propagator graphs.
Following from (
357
) then

f^{\rm v}(G,P)=f^{\rm v}(g)\circ P^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,\qquad c^{\rm e}(G,P)=P^{\rm e}{}^{-\frac{1}{2}}c^{\rm e}(g)P^{\rm e}{}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,

(362)

and

g^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(G,P)=G^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(G,P)\circ P^{\prime}(G,P)^{\frac{1}{2}}=g^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g)\circ{\cal R}\,,

(363)

where

g^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g)=\big(g^{\rm v}+f^{\rm v}(g)\big)\circ\big(\mathds{1}-2\,c(g)\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,

(364)

with

{\cal R}^{\rm e}=\big(\mathds{1}^{\rm e}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,P^{\rm e}{\!}{}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,P^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(G,P)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

(365)

From this definition

{\cal R}^{\rm e}\,{\cal R}^{\rm e}{}^{T}=\mathds{1}^{\rm e}\,,

(366)

The inverse of (
363
)

g^{\rm v}(g{\hskip 0.5pt}^{\prime})=\big(g^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}+f^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g{\hskip 0.5pt}^{\prime})\big)\circ\big(\mathds{1}-2\,c^{\prime}(g^{\prime}{\hskip 0.5pt})\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,\quad

(367)

is obtained by taking

\displaystyle f^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g{\hskip 0.8pt}^{\prime})={}

\displaystyle-f^{\rm v}(g)\circ\big(\mathds{1}-c^{\prime}(g{\hskip 0.8pt}^{\prime})\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,

\displaystyle c^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(g{\hskip 0.8pt}^{\prime})={}

\displaystyle\mathds{1}^{\rm e}-\big(\mathds{1}^{\rm e}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g)\big)^{-1}=-\big(\mathds{1}^{\rm e}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g)\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,c^{\rm e}(g)\,\big(\mathds{1}^{\rm e}-c^{\rm e}(g)\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,

(368)

from which it follows that
f^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g{\hskip 0.5pt}^{\prime}),\ c^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(g{\hskip 0.5pt}^{\prime})
are both expressible
as expansions in
g{\hskip 0.5pt}^{\prime}
in terms of contributions corresponding to 1PI diagrams. For
f,\,c

infinitesimal the generator of reparametrisations can be reduced, from (
363
), (
366
), to the form

{\cal D}_{f,c}(G,P)={\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle f+(g\hskip 0.5ptc)$}(g)+{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle(g\hskip 0.5pt\omega)$}(g)\,,\quad{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle h$}(g)=\sum_{\rm v}h^{\rm v}(g)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial g^{\rm v}}\,,

(369)

where

{\cal R}^{\rm e}=\mathds{1}^{\rm e}+\omega^{\rm e}\,,\qquad\omega^{\rm e}{}^{T}=-\omega^{\rm e}\,.

(370)

Under a reparametrisation

A^{\prime}(G^{\prime},P^{\prime})=A(G,P)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad A^{\prime}(g{\hskip 0.8pt}^{\prime})=A(g)\,,

(371)

is consistent with (
357
).

The essential RG functions
{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(G,P),\ \gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)
are formed from contributions
corresponding to 1PI diagrams, with
\gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)
symmetric. Corresponding to (
358
)

{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(G\circ{\cal R},{\cal R}^{T}P\,{\cal R})={\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(G,P)\circ{\cal R}\,,\quad\gamma^{\rm e}(G\circ{\cal R},{\cal R}^{T}P\,{\cal R})={\cal R}^{\rm e}{}^{T}\gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)\,{\cal R}^{\rm e}\,.

(372)

Reduction to the coupling
g
, determined as in (
357
), is achieved by taking

{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(G,P)={\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(g)\circ P^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,\qquad\gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)=P^{\rm e}{}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gamma^{\rm e}(g)P^{\rm e}{}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,

(373)

and then
{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(g),\,\gamma^{\rm e}(g)
are expressible
just in terms of 1PI contributions. RG flow is then generated by

{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}},\gamma$}(G,P)\,,

(374)

where

{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}},\gamma$}(G,P)\,A(g)={\cal D}_{\beta}(g)\,A(g)\,,\qquad\beta^{\rm v}(g)={\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(g)+(g^{\rm v}\gamma(g))\,,

(375)

where we make use of

{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}},\,\gamma$}(G,P)\,P^{\rm e}{}^{\frac{1}{2}}=P^{\rm e}{}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\gamma^{\rm e}+\omega^{\rm e})=(\gamma^{\rm e}-\omega^{\rm e})P^{\rm e}{}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,\ \ \mbox{for some}\ \ \omega^{\rm e}=-\omega^{\rm e}{}^{T}\,.

(376)

and

{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle(g\hskip 0.5pt\omega)$}(g)\,A(g)=0\,.

(377)

In (
375
) the
\beta
-function
\beta^{\rm v}(g)
then has the standard
form in terms of 1PI contributions and from (
358
)

Under a reparametrisation as in (
361
)

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(G^{\prime},P^{\prime})={}

\displaystyle{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}},\gamma$}(G,P)\,G^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(G,P)\,,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(G^{\prime},P^{\prime})={}

\displaystyle-{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}},\gamma$}(G,P)\,P^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(G,P)^{-1}=\gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)+{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}},\gamma$}(G,P)\,c^{\rm e}(G,P)\,.

(378)

Defining

{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(G^{\prime},P^{\prime})\circ P^{\prime}{}^{\frac{1}{2}}={\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g^{\prime})\circ{\cal R}\,,\qquad P^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}{}^{\frac{1}{2}}\gamma^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(G^{\prime},P^{\prime})\circ P^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}{}^{\frac{1}{2}}={\cal R}^{\rm e}{}^{T}\gamma^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(g^{\prime})\,{\cal R}^{\rm e}\,,

(379)

then reduces, with
{\cal R}
given by (
365
), for
{\tilde{\beta}}(g),\ \gamma(g)
to

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g^{\prime})={}

\displaystyle\big({\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(g)+{\cal D}_{\beta}(g)\,f^{\rm v}(g)-\big(f_{\rm v}(g)\gamma(g)\big)\big)\circ(\mathds{1}-2\,c(g))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(g^{\prime})={}

\displaystyle(\mathds{1}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\gamma^{\rm e}(g)+{\cal D}_{\beta}(g)\,c^{\rm e}(g)-\big\{\gamma^{\rm e}(g),c^{\rm e}(g)\big\}\big)(\mathds{1}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,

(380)

To achieve (
380
) we make use of

{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle(g\hskip 0.5pt\omega)$}(g)\,f^{\rm v}(g)=(f^{\rm v}(g)\hskip 0.5pt\omega)\,,\qquad{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle(g\hskip 0.5pt\omega)$}(g)\,c^{\rm e}(g)=\big[c^{\rm e}(g),\omega\big]\,.

(381)

The expressions obtained in (
380
)
ensure that
{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g^{\prime}),\,\gamma^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(g^{\prime})
expanded in terms
of
g^{\prime}
as in (
363
) are expressible in terms of 1PI contributions and furthermore

\beta^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g^{\prime})={\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(g^{\prime})+\big(g^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}\gamma(g^{\prime})\big)={\cal D}_{\beta}(g)\,g^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}+(g^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}\,\Omega)\,,\quad\Omega=-\Omega^{T}\,,

(382)

where

\displaystyle\Omega^{\rm e}=(\mathds{1}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big([c^{\rm e}(g),\gamma^{\rm e}(g)]+{\hat{\Omega}}^{\rm e}\big)(\mathds{1}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal D}_{\beta}(g)(\mathds{1}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ (\mathds{1}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g))^{\frac{1}{2}}=(\mathds{1}-2\,c^{\rm e}(g))^{-1}\big({\cal D}_{\beta}(g)\,c^{\rm e}(g)-{\hat{\Omega}}^{\rm e}\big)\,.

(383)

From (
378
)

{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}$}(G,P)=\exp\big(-{\cal D}_{v,w}(G,P)\big)\,{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}},\gamma$}(G,P)\exp\big({\cal D}_{v,w}(G,P)\big)\,,

(384)

or

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}{}^{\prime}(G,P)={}

\displaystyle\exp\big({-{\cal L}_{v,w}(G,P)}\big){\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(G,P)\,,\quad\gamma^{\rm e}{}^{\prime}(G,P)=\exp\big({-{\cal L}_{v,w}(G,P)}\big)\gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)\,,

(385)

with, to lowest order,

\displaystyle\delta{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(G,P)=-{\cal L}_{v,w}(G,P)\,{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(G,P)={}

\displaystyle{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}},\gamma$}(G,P)\,v^{\rm v}(G,P)-{\cal D}_{v,w}(G,P)\,{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(G,P)\,,

\displaystyle\delta\gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)=-{\cal L}_{v,w}(G,P)\,\gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)={}

\displaystyle{\cal D}\raisebox{-1.5pt}{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{\beta}},\gamma$}(G,P)\,w^{\rm e}(G,P)-{\cal D}_{v,w}(G,P)\,\gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)\,.

(386)

For application here we set up a basis of 1PI vertex and propagator graphs so that

\displaystyle v^{\rm v}(G,P)=\sum_{\ell,r}\,\epsilon_{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}\,{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{p_{{\rm v}\ell r}}$}\,G^{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}(G,P)\,,\qquad w^{\rm e}(G,P)=\sum_{\ell,r}\,\epsilon_{{\rm e}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}\,{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{p_{{\rm e}\ell r}}$}\,G^{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm e}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}(G,P)\,.

(387)

In the expansion of
v^{\rm v}(G,P)
the sum is over contributions

G^{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}(G,P)
corresponding to particular
\ell
,
\ell=1,2,\dots
,
loop 1PI vertex graphs
{\cal G}^{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}
, with the same external lines as
{\rm v}
, and labelled by
r
.
In each case
{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{p_{{\rm v}\ell r}}$}
denotes the sum over the
p_{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}

permutations of the external lines of
{\cal G}^{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}
necessary to ensure the symmetry under external line
permutations satisfied by
G^{\rm v}
. Similarly
G^{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm e}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}(G,P)
corresponds
to a 1PI propagator graph
{\cal G}^{{\rm e}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}
,
the associated permutations over external lines are

{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{p_{{\rm e}\ell r}}$}
with
p_{{\rm e}\ell r}=1,\,2

according to whether
{\cal G}^{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm e}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}
is symmetric or not.
Inserting vertex or propagator graphs generates an algebra which arises from

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hskip 0.5ptp_{{\rm v}^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}}}$}\,G^{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm v}^{\prime}\hskip 0.5pt\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}}(G,P)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial G}\ {\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hskip 0.5ptp_{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm g}\ell r}}$}\,G^{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm g}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}(G,P)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\,N_{{\rm g}\hskip 1.0ptLs}^{{\rm v}^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}\!,\,{\rm g}\hskip 0.8pt\ell r}\,{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hskip 0.5ptp_{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm g}Ls}}$}\,G^{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm g}\hskip 0.5ptLs}(G,P)\,,

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hskip 0.5ptp_{{\rm e}^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}}}$}\,G^{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm e}^{\prime}\hskip 0.5pt\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}}(G,P)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial P^{-1}}\ {\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hskip 0.5ptp_{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm g}\ell r}}$}\,G^{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm g}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}(G,P)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\,N_{{\rm g}\hskip 1.0ptLs}^{{\rm e}^{\prime}\hskip 0.8pt\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}\!,\,{\rm g}\hskip 0.8pt\ell r}\,{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{\hskip 0.5ptp_{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm g}Ls}}$}\,G^{\hskip 0.5pt{\rm g}\hskip 0.5ptLs}(G,P)\,,

\displaystyle{\rm g}\in\{{\rm v},\,{\rm e}\}\,,\quad L=\ell^{\prime}+\ell\,.

(388)

In general
N_{{\rm g}\hskip 1.0ptLs}^{{\rm g}^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}\!,\,{\rm g}\hskip 0.8pt\ell r}
are integers.

Just as in (
387
) there is a similar expansion

\displaystyle{\tilde{\beta}}^{\rm v}(G,P)=\sum_{\ell,r}\,\beta_{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}\,{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{p_{{\rm v}\ell r}}$}\,G^{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}(G,P)\,,\qquad\gamma^{\rm e}(G,P)=\sum_{\ell,r}\,\gamma_{{\rm e}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}\,{\mathcal{S}}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle{p_{{\rm e}\ell r}}$}\,G^{{\rm e}\hskip 0.5pt\ell r}(G,P)\,.

(389)

The results in (
386
) then ensure

\displaystyle\delta\alpha_{{\rm g}\hskip 0.5ptLs}=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{3}{\gv'r'\ell',r\hskip 0.5pt\ell}{\ell'+\ell=L}}\hskip-2.84544ptn_{{\rm g}^{\prime}}\hskip 2.84544pt{}

\displaystyle N_{{\rm g}Ls}^{{\rm g}^{\prime}\hskip 0.8pt\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}\!,\,{\rm g}\hskip 0.8pt\ell r}\;X_{{\rm g}^{\prime}\hskip 0.8pt\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}\!,\,{\rm g}\hskip 0.8pt\ell r}\,,\quad\alpha_{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5ptLs}=\beta_{{\rm v}\hskip 0.5ptLs}\,,\ \alpha_{{\rm e}\hskip 0.5ptLs}=\gamma_{{\rm e}\hskip 0.5ptLs}\,,\ n_{{\rm v}^{\prime}}=1,\,n_{{\rm e}^{\prime}}=2\,,

\displaystyle X_{{\rm g}^{\prime}\hskip 0.8pt\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}\!,\,{\rm g}\hskip 0.8pt\ell r}=\alpha_{{\rm g}^{\prime}\hskip 0.8pt\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}}\ \epsilon_{{\rm g}\hskip 0.8pt\ell r}-\epsilon_{{\rm g}^{\prime}\hskip 0.8pt\ell^{\prime}r^{\prime}}\ \alpha_{{\rm g}\hskip 0.8pt\ell r}\,.

(390)

This is then equivalent to (
221
) and (
222
) used in section
10
.
